About the Town
SETTLED:
1718
INCORPORATED:
August 1, 1728
TYPE OF GOVERNMENT:
Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend on
the north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and Lancaster
on the south, and Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It is
about 47 miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.
COUNTY:
Worcester
ELEVATION AT TOWN HALL:
Approximately 570 ft. above mean sea level

Board of Selectmen
SKERWOOD G. WINCHESTER
RUDOLPH MASELLA
ANN P. HALL
Board of Selectmen: left to right - Sherwood G. Winchester, Chairman Rudolph Masella and Ann P. Hall.
All Town Officials spent much time in 1977 at what has become the perennial task of sorting through the maze
of regulations, requirements, and restrictions formulated by higher governmental authority. The Board of Selectmen
dealt with their share of the sorting, together with the regular duties of the office, during the 38 regular meetings,
2 special meetings, and numerous other meetings with other Boards and Committees at the local and regional level,
and on site inspections.
Although electionsby their very nature bring about changes, this year's seems to have been noteworthy on two
counts: one, the retirement of Normand J. Thompson after fifteen years of service a period of great growth and
change in Town government -- during which his cumulative knowledge and experience was of great value to this Board
and to the Town; and two, the election of the first woman to the Office of Selectman.
The proposal for the construction of a sewerage system in the Whalom area found the Board spending much time
on preparation for holding of a Public Hearing and a Special Town Meeting, the details of which are covered else-
where in this report.
Acting in the capacity of the Local Licensing Authority, 17 licenses were issued for the sale of alcoholic bev-
erages; 53 one-day special licenses were issued; and 9 public hearings were held in connection with licensing mat-
ters. It was a particularly busy year for Board of Health matters. Among the more far-reaching was the change in
the Town Nursing Service. The resignation of Mrs. Janet N. Pace, combined with the preliminary draft of regula-
tions for extended nursing service coverage, prompted the Board of Health to join the Nashoba Nursing Service. Mrs.
Shirley M. Carpenter was employed by Nashoba to serve Lunenburg providing continuity to our patients despite the
change. The school nurses will be in the direct employ of the School Department for the future.
A "Final Decision ordering the Town to comply with the regulations for the operation and closure of the Lun-
enburg Municipal Refuse Disposal Area was received from the Massachusetts Department of Environmental Quality
Engineering. In orderto comply with the orderto coverall refuse daily andstill keepthe taxpayer's cost within rea-
son, the Board of Health was forced with regret to reduce the number of days of operation at the MRDA. We are
pleased to report that the DEQE is satisfiedwith the Town's efforts in this direction and have under review the Clo-
sure Plan which was submitted on November fifteenth. The Refuse Disposal Planning Committee and the Selectmen
are working closely together on the search for a new site, and their recommendations will be brought before the Town
sometime during 1978.
- 4 -
===PAGE 7===
The preceding matters and many, many more have involved the long hours and hard work of many of our citi-
zens. The Board of Selectmen want to thank each and every one who has participated in the workings of Town gov-
ernment. It ishoped that our young people will take notice of this vital example of citizenshipas practiced by these
many dedicated volunteers.
As the year draws to a close, we are preparing a proclamation for the New Year's Day ceremony initiating Lun-
enburg's 250th Anniversary Observance. This gives US pause to reflect over our long and varied past: "Lunenburg -
The Heritage of Turkey Hills", a history of our Town, published in December by the Lunenburg Historical Society is
an invaluable resource for the study of the people and events which shaped the Town as we know it. The Board of
Selectmen urges everyone to join in the celebration of our Town's birthday, and hopes that all Lunenburg residents
will take advantage of this special opportunity to see one another, meet some of your neighbors you haven't met be-
fore, and together enjoy this wonderful Town we all call home.
Planning Board
The primary work of the Planning Board in 1977 consisted of: A. The preparation, presentation and adoption
at the Annual Town Meeting of the 80,000 square foot lot area in the Watershed/Residence B section of Town; B.
The processing of one new residential subdivision containing ten lots; C. The adoption of the new subdivision rules
and regulations; D. Processing of 73 new lots not requiring subdivision approval as required by Chapter 40A; and E.
Preliminary work on updating the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg.
A complete recodification of the Protective By-law was necessitated to insure compliance with Chapter 808 of
the Acts of 1975 as mandated by the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. All cities and towns must find themselves in
compliance by July 1978. To this end the Lunenburg Planning Board at the time of this report is taking steps to re-
view and organize the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg for presentation at the 1978 Annual Town Meeting.

Board of Assessors
The Board of Assessors wishes to report that 1977 was anotherslow year for new construction. The majority of
construction during the past year has been additions to existing housing and additional outbuildings. We would es-
timate the additional valuation coming into being during 1977 to be in the area of three quarters of a million dollars.
One large parcel of landwhich is now tax exempt is the former property and buildings owned by the Fitchburg
Railroad Company which is located on Summer Street. This property was taken by the Massachusetts Bay Transporta-
tion Authority (MBTA) on December 24, 1976. The property had been valued at $310,100.00 and was taxed for
$14,884.80. This is now exempt property and the tax money is lost to the Town.
The Board of Assessors is again this year requesting funds through an Article to be inserted in the Warrant at
the forthcoming Annual Town Meeting. We wish to change our present method of recording land transactions in the
Assessors' Office and acquiring topographical maps made of a size to be usable in the field. Our problem is that the
aerial maps now being used to show property lines are becoming worn from twenty-seven years of use. Each sale is
now drawn on to the existing photographs by hand in ink, and the lots are described by the owner's name and area of
the tract of land.
We propose to have topographical maps drawn from our photomaps, reduced to a manageable size and a new
numerical system of recording ownership established. Each tract of land in Lunenburg would have a number, a card
file would show the owner's name. In the event of a sale, a new card would be filled out and the change would be
complete. The existing photomaps would be available for useby the public and the other departments for the viewing
of property, but will not be maintained as to future changes in property lines. We do trust that the voters of the
Town will agree with our request and vote affirmatively on this Article at the Annual Town Meeting in April 1978.
We wish to report on new laws pertaining to exemptions that have been recently changed or are now in the
works at the State House in Boston. We wish to advise veterans with V.A. certified disabilities who are additionally
certified as 100% unemployable, that they may qualify for a greater exemption under SECTION 22E. The State Leg-
islature has now rewritten the State laws dealing with the several exemptions available to the taxpayerswho qualify,
raising some of the maximum amounts that a taxpayer may have in order to qualify for these exemptions.
The Board of Assessors wishes to remind taxpayers of certain exemptions that have been enacted concerning the
value of residential property, owned and occupied as a domicile by persons in various groups for whom the payment
of taxes could constitute an undue burden. These exemptions may not exceed specific amounts. If a taxpayer feels
that he qualifies for any of the following exemptions, he should contact the Assessors' Office and request an appli-
cation which lists all the requirements that must be met in order to qualify.
Section 17 - Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation or a tax of
$175, whichever is greater.
Section 22 - Disabled or wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000 valuationor
a tax from $175 to $875, whichever is greater depending on degree of disability.
Section 22E - Veterans with yearly certificate from Veterans' Administration indicating 100% disability.
Section
37
- Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Section
41
- Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation or a tax of $350, whichever is greater. Some of these
amounts are in the process of being changed by the State Legislature.
Section 41A - Tax deferral for certainpersons 65 years of age orover with gross receipts of under $20,000 annually
toplace the taxes onhis domicile and one acre of land on a deferred payment plan, the taxes to be
paid upon settlement of the estate plus 8% interest.
Section
42
- Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valuation or
a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Section 43 - Minor orphansof police officers or firefighterskilled in theline of duty - $8,000 valuation or a tax
of $700, whichever is greater.
Clause 61A
Section 9
- Provides for assessment of agricultural or horticultural land at value based upon its agricultural or
horticultural use.

Park Commission
The 1977 Summer Program was a huge success under the leadership of our Director, Richard Stachowicz. He
was ably assisted by Karen Upton at the Center Playground and Tim Mulligan at the Wallis Park Playground. Both of
these playgrounds were very well attended because of the quality and enthusiasm of both instructors. We should also
not fail to mention the fine Arts and Crafts Program conducted by Cindy St. Arnaud and Joan Carey. The Town Beach
was very ably directed by Gerry Beloin. He and his staff are to be commended for the quality of their Swim Instruc-
tion and Life-Saving Programs. The Town Beach is batting 100% as far as serious accidents are concerned none!
John Therrien has done a great deal to promote tennis in our Town. In spite of court conditions, he instructed
143 people last summer. This figure can be broken down as follows: 9-13 years of age 65; High School 18; Ad-
ults - 30; Whalom (Youth and Adult) - 30. Chuck Winneberg led the girls' softball teams to a most successful season.
More girls are becoming involved in this program each year. The men's Softball League had a very good year with
five teams being represented.
We would like to commend the Personnel Board for their granting of our request for increased pay for Playground
Supervisors in order to be able to be more competitive with the area. One of our projects for this year is the setting
up of a master calendar. Each group which uses our facilities during the spring and summer will be asked to submit
their schedule to US in advance. There are so many demands placed uponour existing facilitiesthat we feel a calen-
dar is very much called for, in order that four teams will not try to practice or play agame on the same field at the
same time.
If the joint Conservation/Recreation Plan gets a priority rating from Boston, we plan to carry out a number of
programs within aperiod of fiveyears. First Year: To bringour three tennis courts up to satisfactory condi-
tion and to increase the number of courts to a minimum of five (maximum seven). The Town ownsthis land already,
and the Commission would like to build additional courts in Whalom and in the Center. Second Year: To turn the
landfill area at Hickory Hills into arecreational complex baseball field, playground equipment, etc. Third Year:
To improve our recreational offerings at Shirley Reservoir. We plan to have a skating area as well as establishing
several cross-country ski trails. Fourth Year: To take into consideration at each of our new facilities, the recrea-
tional needs of the elderly. During this fourth year we would take a look at all our facilities and programs to see if
we can provide for the needs of the elderly. Opportunities to be offered are shuffleboard, horseshoes, playing cards,
etc. Fifth Year: To erect a building for Arts and Crafts in the Whalom area. The youngsters deserve and need this
type of program. We would also like to addto some of our existing playground equipment and replace other outdated
equipment. Our concern is for Kindergarten through upper Elementary.
If you look very closely at our five year action plan, you will find that in this plan are represented the re-
creational needs of the Townspeople from one to one hundred years of age. With your support at the Annual Town
Meeting, this proposed five year plan will be able to be put into ACTION.
Our Commission would like to thank all who in any way helped to make our programs so successful. Special
thanks to Town Clerk, Mary Padula, and her very able staff who have given US their invaluable assistance whenever
we needed it. Last, but certainly far from least a very special thank you to our Supervisor, Robert Garneau and
his able assistant, Greg Bingham, who have kept our facilities in excellent condition. They certainly have a love
for their Town. Bob can be seen on a Sunday clearing the ice on Marshall Pond this is real dedication.
In closing, the Commissioners would like to appeal to the Townspeople to support US at the Annual Town Meet-
ing. We feel that we have your interests at heart why not have ours at heart.

Cemetery Commission
The Cemetery Commissionersmet on a regular schedule throughout the year to changeor align policy commen-
suratewith current requirements, and to provide the necessary assistance and guidance to the Cemetery Superintendent.
Administrative forms for the sale, return and transfer of burial lots were updated to provide positive agreements be-
tween the Town of Lunenburg and lot grantees:
The current inventory of equipment was adequately repaired and maintained, and no major equipment purchases
were required. Both cemetery grounds were seeded, fertilized and groomed as required throughout the year and
broken free debris was removed to insure proper growth and appearance.
The repair of old or broken monuments was especially important in the continuing maintenance program to pre-
serve the appearance of the cemeteries, and to prepare for additional visitors expected during the Town's forthcoming
250th Anniversary celebration.

Library Trustees
Library Trustees: Seated left to right - Barbara W. Brown, Barbara T. Murray and Beverly A. Mulholland. Standing
left to right - Librarian John E. Ramsay, John J. Boursy and Janet M. Cragin. Not shown is Robert S. Tapply.
Several major projects designed to improve library services and better utilize existing spacehave been accom-
plishedat the Ritter Memorial Library during the year 1977. The layout of the Library has been completely rearranged
to make it more convenient and functional for its patrons. The children now have a separate area on the lower level
of the building, which gives them a place for their own programs removed from the flow of adult services, and also
enables the Children's Librarian to devote all her energies to serving the younger members of the community.
The main entrance level now contains the card catalog and copier machine, along with the reference collec-
tion. The most arduous part of the moving involved the addition of new wooden shelving for the fiction collection,
now also located on the main level. This project was undertaken and completed by Librarian John Ramsay and Library
Assistant Chris Mulholland who, with funds for materials being furnished from Library Trust Funds and the Friends of
Ritter Memorial Library, and the greatly appreciated use of the woodworking facilities of Lunenburg High School,
managed to design and construct the shelves at a 95% savings to the Town.
Locating the reference and fiction collections, plus the copier machine and card catalog, on the entrance
level makes these materials accessible to all Lunenburg residents, especially to the elderly and handicapped who
before found it difficult or impossible to avail themselves of the Library's facilities. Furthermore, this relocation
assures that there will always be a staff member nearby tohelp patrons use any of these sources. This total reorgan-
ization has involved a lot of hard work on the part of the staff and temporary inconvenience to Library users, but
should enable the Ritter Memorial Library to continue to service Lunenburg effectively for many years to come.
Despite ongoing changes and occasional confusion, business has remained steady at the Library. The circulation
of adult materials kept pace with last year's figures which had shown a 16% increase over the past five years. Adult
totals for 1977 are: Books 18,838 (109 interlibrary loans), Phonograph Records 960, Magazines 396, Pamphlets 13,
and Talking Books 3. Film circulation (71) dropped off because of stricter lending policies imposed by the regional
library system. Use of children's materials has shown a large increase over the past two years, with 1976 rising 7%
and 1977 up another 10%. The figures for children's circulation are: Books 24,571, Magazines 210, Audio-Visual
Materials 199, Puzzles 59 and Records 510. Obviously, even during all the shifting of materials, the residents of
Lunenburg were able to get a lot of use from the Ritter Memorial Library.
Circulation figures demonstrate only one part of the total picture for today's libraries. Of perhaps equal im-
portance is the providing of programs noteworthy for their cultural, educational and recreational merits. The Library,
- 10
===PAGE 13===
despite its size and small staff, has been a standout in this
respect over the past year. For children, a major Library
program was the summer reading program for Grades 2-7.
The 1976 session had been a big success, but was surpassed
by a remarkable 1977 program, with a 48% increase in
children who read the designated number of books and a
52% rise in books read. These statistics would not have
been so without a lively schedule of events over the
summer months, including two movies, a magic show, a
performance by the Little Red Wagon travell group from
the University of New Hampshire (sponsored by the Friends
of the Library), and a representative from the Massach-
usetts Audubon Society's Druml in Farmwho introduced the
children and their parents to several interesting wild ani-
mals. These children's programs made this summer a very
busy one at the Library. Other children's activities in-
cluded preschool story hours which this year had a waiting
list, a Christmas party with movies, guitar sing-along,
refreshments, and stories. The Friends of the Library pro-
vided funds and helpers for several other events, among
which were after-school knitting lessons, Easter egg dec-
orating, making and decorating baked dough plaques, the
Jane Caulfield and friend from Druml in Farm paid US avisit.
Children's Arts Festival, and a Halloween magic show.
In the adult realm, the Library continued its popular Sunday film series, showing in 1977 Captains Courageous,
The Playboy of the Western World, Shoot the Piano Player, Mary of Scotland, Throne of Blood, and The Red Psalm.
The Friends of the Library were responsible for scheduling two adult "Interest Series" with local experts discussing
and demonstrating topics such as yoga, astrology, flower arranging, natural foods, punch needle handwork, patch-
work Christmas ornaments, and cross-country skiing. All sessions drew consistent and diverse audiences who received
the topics with great enthusiasm.
The Friends of the Library have been mentioned frequently in this report, and this is only fitting, considering
the great efforts they have put forth on behalf of the Library. They have helped plan and carry out numerous events
throughout the year, and have also put much energy and hard work into fund-raising projects to enable the Library
to achieve some flexibility despite a fixed and limited budget. Their fund-raising efforts have greatly helped the
Ritter Memorial Library to more than fulfill its role as a cultural and educational center for all our residents.
In conclusion, 1977 witnessed a total revamping of the Ritter Memorial Library in order to increase its shelving
capacities and maximize its services for all of Lunenburg's residents. Overall use of the Library, even during the
uprooting, has increased significantly, and the continued energy of the Library staff and the Friends of the Library
augurs a healthy outlook for years to come.
Jay Janell's magic show delights his audience.

===PAGE 14===
Council on Aging
The year 1977 was a progressive one for the Council on Aging, particularly with the expansion of the "Meals
on Wheels" Program. On July 1st, the "Meals on Wheels" expanded from three to five days a week, serving up to
fifteen elderly shut-ins daily. During the summer the meals were provided by the Montachusett Nutrition Project,
with the School Department supplying them with its reopening in September. Over 1,900 mealswere delivered during
the year by a very devoted group of volunteers who travelled over 4,300 miles. The Council wishes to express their
appreciation to Mrs. Doris Carrier and Mrs. Barbara Jordan for heading up our volunteer program, with added thanks
to Theresa Chapdelaine, Tona Xenakis, Mary Larkin, Mary Taylor, Jane Duval, Eileen Alleva, Dorothy Paradis,
Cathy Moilanen, Marie Gibbs, Edith Carey and Kathy Draskozy for giving so willingly of their time.
We also wish to extend special thanks to the School Committee, the School Lunch Program and Personnel, the
Montachusett Nutrition Project, and the Lunenburg Public Health Nurse, who have been so cooperative in making
this a successful venture. As in all programs of this nature, volunteers are always needed, and we would welcome
anyone interested in serving as volunteer, male or female, to contact Mrs. Barbara Jordan, Flynn Road at 582-6292.
The Council Newsletter has been well received by our 950 Senior Citizens during the year, and trust that we
have made the letter informative to keep them abreast of programs, events, and updates on Social Security, insurance,
etc. The Council praises Mrs. Marcella Peed for doing an excellent job of editing this newsletter. The Montachu-
sett Home Care Corporation continues to be under contract to serve eligible elderly in our Town with homemaker,
chore, and health services. These services, in many cases, make the difference in a person's remaining at home or
having to rely on institutional care.
The Fifth Annual Christmas Dinner was held at the High School, and it was very well attended with 196 din-
ners being served to our Senior Citizens, followed by a delightful musical program directed by Donald Adams. This
coming year the Council will be making a survey and study into the transportation needs for the Town's elderly, mainly
for the provision of medical and shopping trips. Inasmuch as this Town has no access to public transportation, the
Council feels strongly for the need of such a program.
The Council deeply regrets the loss of Council Member Thomas Passios, who passed away in late June, and also
the resignation of Beatrix Donnelly, who had served on the Council for several years and has taken up residence on
Cape Cod. We also wish to extend a welcome to three new Council members: Elvira May, Milton Carpenter and
Barbara Jordan.
S
Housing Authority
The Lunenburg Housing Authority is in its eighth year in the operation of the facilities at 131 White Street known
as Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly. Presently we have forty-two apartments occupied by single persons and six
apartments occupied by couples. We have presently on file twelve applications from persons who are seeking admis-
sion should a vacancy occur.
The yearly income limits for eligibility have been increased from $4,200 to $6,000 for a single person and from
$4,800 to $6,300 for couples.
The tenants have their own organized group, known as the Pearl Brook Tenants' Association, which meets on a
regular basis and arranges interesting programs and projects for the benefit and enjoyment of its members. Mrs. Mil-
dred Sprowson held the presidency of Pearl Brook Association until November 1977. She was succeeded by Mrs. Helen
Byron who presently holds the office of President.
One of our original tenants, Mrs. Georgia M. Brown, who celebrated her 100th birthday while she was a resi-
dent at the Pearl Brook Housing, passed away in November 1977. She had been a life-long resident of the Town of
Lunenburg and will be sadly missed by all.

Town By-law Committee
Town By-law Committee - left to right: Chairman David W. Rodgers, Aime R.
Denault, Jr., Paulette C. Beardmore, Scott A. Bowron and Elliott M. Zide.
The Town By-law Committee was established in accordance with Article 7 of the 1977 Annual Town Meeting.
Per vote of the Town under said Article 7, the Board of Selectmen and Moderator appointed five members, voters at
large of Lunenburg, to serve on this Committee for the purpose of reviewing, updating and recommending to the Town
changes to the Town By-laws established pursuant to Section 21 of Chapter 40 of the General Laws.
The Town By-law Committee held its organizational meeting in May of 1977 and have met throughout the year
and have deliberated with department heads and various Town officials in order to carry out the vote of the Towns-
people. The Committee will report to the voters at the 1978 Annual Town Meeting, at which time they will present
their findings and recommendations.

Tree Warden
One hundred three diseased trees were cut, chipped
and buried. The State Department of Natural Resources
gave five days ($600.00) of tree removal work and two days
of tagging trees suspected of having Dutch Elm disease.
These samples were sent to the University of Mass-
achusetts for analysis. The Town was reimbursed $508.61
by the County. A total of $9,644.00 was spent under the
Dutch Elm Program.
Seventeen trees were purchased and planted along
various streets. Work under the Tree Warden's supervision
amounted to $6,475.00
The Tree Warden wishes to remind residents that most
pesticides are poisonous. Spray only when pest control is
Richard W. Brown, Tree Warden
essential. Read and follow all directions carefully.

250th Anniversary Committee
250th Anniversary Committee left to right: William R. Proctor, Muriel J. Francis, Chairman Raymond G.
MacCracken, Mary A. Bingham, Robert J. Ebersole, Jean E. Hobbs, David L. Porterand Margaret P. Harley.
The year 1977 for the 250th Anniversary Committee was a year for finalizing many of the plans made since the
Committee wasformed in 1973. The history of the Town: "Lunenburg - The Heritage of Turkey Hills" has been pub-
lished and is available for purchase. Authors are Nelde K. Drumm and Margaret P. Harley, assisted by a commit-
fee from the Historical Society. No one knows the amount of hours that were spent on this project, and the people
of Lunenburg are most fortunate to have such a history completed as part of the 250th Anniversary.
A plaque with the Town Seal has been hung in the Commemorative Arch on the Center Common. As part of the
programduring 1978, abuttonwood treewill be planted on the same Common, in place of the dying mapletree. Com-
memorative plates and tiles, using a design by Eric Niebel especially for the 250th Anniversary, are on sale. Cal-
endars, with pen and ink drawings of old photographs done by Donald Demers and Tonya Cassano, are also on sale
along with bumper stickers and key chains. Proceeds fromthe sales will helpdefray part of the celebration expenses.
Under the sponsorship of the Lunenburg High School Alumni Association, commemorative coins havebeen minted, and
proceeds will benefit alumni programs and scholarships.
A pageant "Under the Buttonwood Tree" has been written by Mari Lucier and will be directed by Janet Cra-
gin. Cast and crew recruiting is in progress, with production dates slated for August 4th and 5th 1978. Lunenburg
has many talented people, and the 250th Anniversary Committee has been able to draw from this talent. People not
already involved in some phase of the 1978 activities are asked to contact any member of the Committee, as there
are many jobs yet to be filled.
The Anniversary Year opened on January 1, 1978, with the issuance of a Proclamation by the Board of Select-
men. The schedule of events for Lunenburg's 250th Anniversary Celebration in 1978 is on the next page.

Conservation Commission
The major portion of the Conservation Commission's time was spent implementing the Commonwealth's Wetland
Protection Act. Neither snow, nor rain, nor heat, nor gloom of night stays the Commission from making Wetlands
site inspections and determinations. To this end the Conservation Commission held 21 regular meetings, 3 special
meetings, 5 public hearings, and acted upon 13 "Requests for Determination". While this is atime-consuming part of
the Commission's job, the members feel that it is better handled at a local level. The interests of the citizens of our
Town are better protected by local people than by some slow-moving bureaucratic office of the State Government.
One of the highlights of the year was providing abuilding on the "Laurelbank Farm Conservation Area" for the
Lunenburg High School "Project Green Thumb" and cooperating with the School Department in making available con-
servation land for our young people who participate in this valuable experience.
The Commonwealth has recently notified the Lunenburg Conservation Commission that a check in the amount of
$12,500 from the "Self-Help Program" will soon be sent to the Town of Lunenburg to reimburse the Town 50% of the
purchase price of the "Laurelbank Farm Conservation Area". Because of Federal and State requirements for a Con-
servation/Recreation Plan, the Conservation Commission and Park Commission have worked together this year to im-
plement a Conservation/Recreation Plan for the Town of Lunenburg. This Plan will make Lunenburg eligible for fu-
ture "Self-Help" funds. However, because of new regulations about population density and income levels, the Com-
mission expects that such funds will be severely limited for suburban and rural communities, such as Lunenburg.
The Massachusetts Legislature has traditionally acknowledged the importance of wetland protection, and this
year recognized the need to conserve our valuable resource of arable land by passing the Farmland Protection Act.
The Conservation Commission feels our local farm people should take advantage of this Act to insurethat our valuable
farmland will be here for future agricultural use.
Lunenburg has long recognized the need to acquire land for conservation to protect our heritage. Your Com-
mission believes it is good business for the Town to purchase more land for conservation when sensible opportunities
arise, and urges Townspeople to support all Town Meeting Articles for land acquisitions.
Worcester
1
Conservation Commission: Seated left to right - Harold D. Harley and Chairman Raymond G. Chapdelaine. Stand-
ing left to right - William H. Naylor, Nancy 1. Sholl, Russell N. Harvey, John C. Lahti and Edward H. Hall, Jr.

Town Forest Committee
The year 1977 has been busier than usual for
this Committee. We have been both discouraged
and pleased. Early in the year, a sturdy gate was
erected for the third time at the entrance to the
main Town Forest on Gilchrest Street to discourage
the road being used at night by automobiles. No
sooner had Mr. Webster installed his handsome
sign on this gate -- it was smashed last summer.
A vote of thanks should be given to the fam-
ily of John Donovan, who live across from this en-
trance. They open and lock the gate for the Town
every day, and report any suspicious actions to the
Police Department. This service was freely offered
and freely given; every Town Committee should be
so fortunate! The gate has again been replaced;
hope springs eternal?
We are very proud of the Jenks Lot on Bur-
rage Street and Flat Hill Road. This has always
been attractive in winter and summer, with high
ridges debouching a hiker or cross-country skier
down towards Lake Shirley. This winter, the Town-
owned Jenks Lot alone has had imaginative care
given to its acres by the Project Green Thumb
students. Many new trails have been laid out;
signs and log bridges erected. Such activities
have taught these students forestry and conserva-
tion in general. The Town of Lunenburg has ben-
efited by the opening up to its citizens of the beau-
Town Forest Committee: left to right - Harold D. Harley,
tiful acres for all of US to enjoy.
Armas S. J. Heino and Chairman Barbara W. Brown.
The Committee has discussed at length the ethic governing use of our Town Forest whether to remove dead
wood, or to leave it as a cover for birds and animals, or to decompose to fertilizer. It was finally voted not to re-
move any wood from these forests. The emphasis of our philosophy should always be, not on keeping the stove filled,
but on learning what happens to wood in its natural environment. We solicit and welcome any suggestions or questions
from Lunenburg citizens; we are all so lucky to share these beautiful forests!

Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
During the year 1977, there were 107 Plumbing and
Gas Permits issued as follows: 10 Plumbing Permits for
commercial buildings; 59 Plumbing Permits for new homes
and remodeled dwellings; 5 Gas Permits for commercial
buildings; 33 Gas Permits for new and remodeled dwell-
ings.
The Plumbing Code is founded upon certain princi-
ples of environmental sanitation and safety through prop-
erly designed, acceptably installed, and adequately main-
tained plumbing systems.
Some of the details of plumbing construction may
vary, but the basic sanitary and safety principles desir-
able and necessary to protect the health of people are the
same everywhere. As interpretations may be required, and
as unforseen situations arise, which are not specifically
covered in the Plumbing Code, the final interpretation
shall be made by the Board of State Examiners of Plumbers.
Nothing in the Plumbing Code shall be construed
as applying to refrigerating, heating, cooling, ventila-
ting or sprinkler fire protection systems beyond the point
where only connection is made with the portable water
distributing system, or with sanitary water or special wastes
drainage systems. Such connections shall be made sub-
ject to a permit, along with the inspection and approval
Thomas E. Mortis
of the Inspector of Plumbing.
Inspector of Weights & Measures
Lunenburg residents have the right to expect that
whatever they buy is measured or weighed accurately -
to assurethis accuracy is the responsibility of the Inspector
of Weights and Measures.
Scales, gasoline pumps, fuel oil truck meters, other
commercially used weighing/measuring devices are reg-
ularly tested for accuracy once a year and unannounced
spot checks are frequently made. Of the weighing and
measuring devices inspected in 1977, 70 were found to be
correct and sealed, 11 scales and pumps were adjusted,
and 5 were not sealed.
In accordancewith Section 41, Chapter 98, General
Laws, all inhabitants or persons having usual places of
business within the Town using weighing devices for selling
goods, wares, or merchandise, for public weighing, or for
hire or reward, are notified tobring them intobe retested,
adjusted and sealed.
Your Inspector extends his appreciation to various
establishments in Town for the cooperation rendered.
Wayne A. Balcom

Veterans' Services
The year 1977 has been one of frustration and in-
creased expenses. This Department has had to assist unin-
sured accident victims and other extremely ill veterans, and
this has caused a drainon the amounts appropriated for our
annual expenditures. Nursing home costs have risen and
medical and professional services have kept up with cost-
of-living -- increasing by at least 5% over last year.
The State Department has issued a memorandum to the
effect that a 3.2% cost-of-living increase be allowed ac-
ross the board. While this may not seem like much of an in-
crease, the overall effect is that the monthly expenses will
rise by at least $100, and this means an increase will have
to be requested in the appropriation for the ensuing year.
We have been very fortunate in that all expenses paid out
so far have been approved by the State, and 50% reimburse-
ment should be forthcoming from State Audit Committee.
This Department once again assisted the elderly in
complying with the Veterans' Administration questionnaire
as to their incomes, etc. and has assisted many veterans in
the course of the year to apply for education loans, home
loans and pensions from the Veterans' Administration. The
Alfred H. Gauthier, Veterans' Agent
year began with a caseload of 21 persons and by the end of
the year, approximately 12 persons had been removed from
the rolls through the help given themby the Veterans' Agent.
This Department has a need for other quarters because the law specifies the requirement for a separate office
with the primary reason being that all correspondence, information, interviews, etc. are of a confidential nature.
The Veterans' Agent wishes to take this opportunity to express his sincere thanks to all Townspeople, and to those
who have assisted his Department over the years.
Personnel Board
The Personnel Board is carrying out its work under increasingly restrictive circumstances, as more laws are
passed on Beacon Hill and in Washington. Minimum wage laws now apply to municipalities, union negotiations and
arbitration procedures are fixed by State Law, and some employee wages are now determined by law.
The Personnel Board, with the patient understanding of many Town employees, has arrived at solutions which
it hopes represent a fair balance between the needs of the taxpayers, and those who provide the many services re-
quired by the Town.
The Selectmen and the Moderator, in their appointments to this Board, have chosen people with widely vary-
ing backgrounds. This has provided the Board with broad experience from which to draw on when considering the
many problems in personnel administration which arise during the year.
This Board has continued its familiar pattern of studying Personnel Plans of other communities, industry and
business wage and benefit levels, and has conducted reviews with Town Department Heads. Information drawn from
these sources provides the background for reaching decisions for employees of the Town and for revisions to the Sal-
ary Administration Plan.

Extension Service
The Lunenburg 4-H Clubs have had another busy year. At present the two most active Clubs are the Outdoor
Adventure Club led by Joyce Downey and Dot Connell, and the Bridle Path 4-H Club with Judy Smith and Dixie
Beck as leaders.
The 4-H Outdoor Adventure Club encourages the enjoyment and appreciation of nature through a variety of
activities. One such activity ishiking which included visits to such places as the Cowdrey Nature Center, Leominster
State Park, Willard Brook, Pearl Hill, the Wapack Trail and Purgatory Chasms. The members planto make their own
backpacks for future hikes. Sliding, skating and a sleigh ride, followed by hot refreshments, are some of the cold
weather activities. In preparation for the strong spring winds, the group is designing and decorating their own kites
and will fly them at Mount Wachusett. The culminating activity of the year will be a weekend camping trip. Formal
meetings wereheld once month led by Dot Connell and Joyce Downey and assisted by Susan Downey. The members
include Bonnie Benedict, Cherie Benham, Kathy Connell, Maura Downey, Nancy Ferguson, Lisa Boyle and Dawn
Misner.
The Bridle Path 4-H Club began the year with a tack cleaning meeting at the home of leader, Judy Smith.
This gavethe new and younger members a chance to learn all about bridles and saddles. Junior leader, Kelly Brandt
gave a presentation on horse judging. Blacksmith Frank Marhefka from Clinton spoke to members on leg and foot prob-
lems. On January 21st, leaders took all interested members to Sterling to hear a program on Fire Prevention and
Safety sponsored by the Sterling 4-H Club.
Visual presentations were given at the February meeting by various members. Our guest speaker was Ginny
Fortier, a Veterinarian Assistant at the Fallon Animal Clinic. Ginny instructed members on veterinarian care and
first-aid for horses. From this we were able to draw up a list of first-aid supplies that all 4-H'ers should keep in
their barn. During the February school vacation, over 20 Bridle Path members traveled with their leaders to an ex-
cellent winter clinic at the Wells Arena in Spencer. Gail McAlpine from Littleton brought her Registered Quarter
Horses and instructed all attending on halter competition and English and Western Equitation.
Our guest speaker in March was Kim Craffey who discussed the Equestrian Program at Worcester State College
and talked about the inter-collegiateshows held throughout the country. A pot luck supperwas held in March at the
Turkey Hill Middle School involving all the Lunenburg 4-H Clubs. Fifteen Bridle Path members gave their visual
presentations. Several members who went on to County competition were Karin Anderson, Dean MacCracken and
Laura Smith who qualified for State level competition at Nashoba Regional High School in Bolton. On March 26th,
Dixie Beck was in charge of a Shot Clinicwith Dr. Fitzgerald. This Clinic is held every year under 4-H sponsorship
and is open to all Lunenburg residents who are horse owners.
Mounted meetings began in April. At the North Leominster Rod and Gun Club, Kelly Brandt, Linda Perrault
and Laura Smith tested the boys and girls on mounted requirements of the incentive test program. A bake sale was
held in April the Searstown Mall with proceeds going towards the Spring Schooling Show Awards. Members attended
a judging clinic at the Westford Fair Grounds as guests of the Middlesex County 4-H Clubs. An exciting photo con-
test was held in April underthe chairmanship of Peter Oulevey and awards were given. Mrs. Archibald Cox was the
judge and instructor for the Spring Schooling Show at the North Leominster Rod & Gun Club. Boys and girls are given
the opportunity each year to learn just what a judge is looking for during the show season.
On June 12th the Open 4-H Horse Show was held at the Rod & Gun Club with Sally Cox as judge. This show
raises money for camp scholarships for Bridle Path members. Over fourteen boys and girls attended the Southern New
England Horse Camp in Spencer this summer and we thank the many Lunenburg businesses who helped by being sponsors
at this show.
The new 4-H year opened in August with a swim party. Yearly awards were given out and new officers were
elected as follows: President, Peter Oulevey; Vice President, Kelly Brandt; Treasurer, Dean MacCracken; Secretary,
Linda Perrault. Junior leaders are Kelly Brandt, Linda Perrault and Laura Smith.
Various events took place during the fall season with many members attending the October 29th Open Horse
Show. The year closed with the Annual Christmas Party where a good time was had by all.

Sewerage & Drainage Investigative Committee
At a Special Town Meeting on May 24, 1977, a Sewerage/Drainage Study Committee was authorized and di-
rected to investigate alternatives to a public sewer system for the Whalom/Bakerville area, The Committee has met 20
times in the nine months since being appointed by the Board of Selectmen in June, 1977. In addition to regular meet-
ings at the Town Hall, we have conducted field tripsto suspected problem areas, heldmeetings with Whalom residents,
andmet with other Town departments for their input on problems and possible solutions, all with a view to getting close
enough to the problems to analyze their magnitude and to begin to see what might logically be done about them.
At the invitation of this Committee, many residents of the Whalom/Bakerville area attended a hearing and
completed a questionnaire. As a result of information gathered, we have determined that there are several types of
problems which require further study, many of which are related to inadequate surface water drainage. Several times
during the year, many residents are bothered by standing water and backed up septic tanks resulting from land satura-
tion. Some of these surface-water problems might possibly be solved by improved maintenance and repair of existing
storm water drains, by minor extensions of these drains to serve low-lying areas not presently connected to the drain-
age systems, by improvement of street grading to direct street water into existing catch basins, and by regrading of
private property by owners, in order to direct rain water run-off into the existing drainage systems.
Many residents have indicated that although their original septic systems, installed before modern health
department standards were in effect, were inadequate and caused problems, installation and proper maintenance of
modern septic systems have provided effective solutions. It appears that many of the still-unsolved problems relating
directly to sewage disposal could also be solved individually, either by installation of new systems or by more effec-
tive and extensive maintenance programs.
Recently, the Environmental Protection Agency has been officially encouraging the use of noncentralized,
low cost treatment technologies in rural and suburban areas, and it is this Committee's feeling that such problems in
the Whalom area as cannot be solved privately might yield to this type of solution. An example would be the instal-
lation of large engineered septic systems on existing vacant land, to serve clusters of homes whose lots cannot accom-
modate adequate private systems. This Committee is recommending that its life be extended for an additional year to
complete investigation of this and other possibilities, and to determine the availability of federal and state monies,
preferably grants, to finance such small-scale solutions.
Sewerage & Drainage Investigative Committee: left to right - David D. Blake, Chester J.
Pliska, Chairman Bernard M. Freeman, William J. Kearney and Mary L. Padula.

Highway Department
Nothing but "bridle paths" served our early set-
tlers and horseback riding was the only way, other than
foot, to pass from place to place. Trails blazed through
the wilderness were the only routes to follow, with
streams to be forded, as there were nobridges for years.
Once housesbegan to appear in Turkey Hills, the
Meeting House erected, and frequent Town Meetings
called, there was a need for better roadways wide en-
ough for carts and wagons which often bogged hubdeep
in mud. Peoplewanted tobe able to travel about to vi-
sit friends in other new towns in the area.
An entry in the records of the Townof Lun-
enburg dated February 13, 1729" describes the original
layout of Northfield Road and Lancaster Avenue. At a
town meeting in 1732 conducted by Sam Page, Modera-
tor, itwas "voted and grantedye sum of thirty-twoshil-
lings to pay Colonel Josiah Willard for eight days serv-
ice done for said town in laying out of highwayes for
time past".
One of the earliest projects, possibly 1724, was
George G. Munyon, Highway Superintendent
cutting of trees and clearing of the way from Lancaster
to the center of town, a portion of which is now Lan-
caster Avenue. Next the course over the hills west to South Fitchburg was cleared. This route started at Main Street
between Town Hall and the Daniel Putnam store (Honey Farms) and continued through "Salt Peter Hollow" passing
Stillman Stone's on Massachusetts Avenue, thence to J. W. Mossman's on what is now Beal Street and by Levi White's
on the present White Street. It was said to have no bridges, nor was it graded, but timber and brush were cut away.
In 1736 Benjamin Bellows and others asked the General Court for 450 acres of land to build the present North-
field Road. It proceeded from Flat Hill passing what is now Massachusetts Avenue and the present Northfield Road,
past Pearl Hill school house, thence to Ashburnham and was to continue to Ticonderoga. The cannon from Fort Ti-
conderoga was brought down over this road by oxen. When they came to a farm that had a yoke of oxen, they put
them on to take the place of those that had made the weary trek.
In the beginning the Selectmen were overseers of highway projects in conjunction with a highway surveyor or
commissioner appointed by them. A copy of a portion of the 1853 town report shows that the practice of tax abate-
ment for work done continued, in addition to paying for extra labor and other work on roads and bridges. (Excerpt
from "The Heritage of Turkey Hills".)
Lunenburg's roads at present are afar cry from wilderness paths, but astime marches on, so does progress. The
following is a report of services performed and projects accomplished on the Town's 76.91 miles of accepted roads:
Chapter 90 Improvement - Mix-in-place surfaces were applied to portions of Lancaster Avenue, Leominster-Shirley
Road, Electric Avenue and Leominster Road. Drainage was installed in Leominster Road for a distance of approxi-
mately 800 feet. Paving work was done on Flat Hill Road, Elmwood Road, Arbor Street, Northfield Road, and New
West Townsend Road. Work under this account cost $38,293.34.
General Highway Maintenance - 400. 10 tons of patch was used on roads all over town at a cost of $15, 111.56 for
labor and materials. Work performed on various roads include: brush cutting, shoulder work, drainage and catch
basin cleaning, roadside mowing, sweeping and gravelling. The purchase of small tools and construction signs also
come out of this account. This portion of General Highway Maintenance cost $30,434.68. Road Oil and Tar was
included in this budget and 33,820 gallons of asphalt costing $18,493.69 was applied to Reservoir Road, New West
Townsend Road, Pleasant Street, Lesure Avenue, Pierce Avenue, Crocker Avenue, Piedmont Avenue, Fish Street,
Turkey Hill Road, Clifton Road, Redwood Road, Rangeley Road and Arbor Street. Under Road Oil and Tar category,
$24,231.00 was spent, making a total of $54,665.68 spent under General Highway Maintenance budget.
- 26 -
===PAGE 29===
Snow Removal The cost of erecting snow fences, plowing, salting and sandingwas $75,614.83. It is the endeavor
of this Department to cut down on the use of salt, and we exercise controlled usage in this respect.
Road Machinery Maintenance - - This account is the hub of all Highway Department operations and includes keeping
the Town's equipment in top repair, painting of equipment, purchase of parts, plow and grader blades, chains, gas,
oil, grease, diesel fuel, acetylene and oxygen. The operation of this Department cost $36,028.77.
Town Barn Maintenance - Heat, light, telephone, office and janitorial supplies, maintenance of office equipment
and special building maintenance cost $6,319.26.
Sick Leave, Vacations, Holidays, Bonus - $14,840.43 was spent under this account.
Special Accounts The reconstruction of a portion of Highland Street cost $15,910.51, including the balance of the
allotment fromthe previous year. Poison Spraying and Brush Cutting cost $1,696.84. Fabrication, Purchase and
Installation of Street Signs cost $493.40. General Maintenance and Repair of Town Roads Article takes the place of
the former Chapter 81 and Chapter 90 Maintenance work which the State has eliminated. Under this Article, minor
drainage, patching, oiling, brush cutting, purchase of sand, sweeping, gravel and gravelling, purchase and instal-
lation of guardrail and loaming and seeding was performed on the Town's roads. A total of 24,037 gallons of asphalt
was applied to Elmwood Road, Arbor Street, Piedmont Avenue, Cross Street, Mulpus Road, West Street, Crocker Ave-
nue, Lesure Avenue, Pierce Avenue, Pleasant Street and Flat Hill Road. Also $45,961.58 was spentunder this ac-
count, including the balance of the allotment from last year. The balance of $369.99 from 1976 was spent on West
Groton Road bridge. Deckingwas replaced, bridge surface replaced and guard rail installed on Townsend Harbor Road
bridge. A total of $13,000 was spent extending the reconstruction of Howard Street. Major drainage was installed
in Sunnyhill Road and Northfield Road. Other roads receiving drainage work were Cross Road, Weatherbee Street,
Baker Street, Reservoir Road, Page Street, Mulpus Road, Gilchrest Street, Chestnut Street, West Townsend Road,
Summer Street and Kingman Street at a cost of $26,777.14. Purchase of a Sander cost $5,499.00. Rubbish Re-
moval cost $1,174.90.
Dog Officer
Approximately 1,600 calls were recorded during 1978 with most calls concerning lost and stray dogs. Licensed
dogs totalled in excess of 1, 200 as a result of the reimpl ementation of the letter program whichwill be continued in
1978. After lengthy investigation and through the utilization of General Laws, numerous troublesome and vicious
animalswere ordered permanently restrained. Many dog problems occur when all family members depart early in the
morning and leave their animals alone, running loose for eight or more hours a day. This office has found that follow-
up calls, and a spirit of cooperation among neighbors, re-
solve many of the problems.
A problem areahasbeen on Flat Hill Road from Sunset
Lane to Reservoir Road--with dogs chasing cars and school
buses, and harrassing pedestrians and residents. Amore in-
dividual effort is needed by dog owners in keeping a closer
check on their animals. Another problem hasbeen the mo-
lesting and destroying of livestock and fowl by dogs causing
injuries serious enough that the livestock had to be destroyed
along with the dog involved resulting in loss of money for
all parties concerned.
The latest communication from the Mass. Dept. of
Natural Resources is as follows: "Keep track of your dog.
Many people believe deer hunting week is the most criti-
cal time for deer. Once they have survived, they are safe
for another year. In fact, the most critical time is the win-
ter, especially when the ground is covered with snow. It
is at such times that dogs pose a threat, as even the most
docile pets chase and often kill deer". We would like to
Audrey J. Misner, Dog Officer
repeat "Keep track of your dog"!

===PAGE 30===
Police Department
The highlight of 1977 was the completion of the new police facility which was occupied in September. We
are now on ground level and the dispatchers can serve everyone who comes in the door, for either police or fire
business, as well as relaying inquiries for other Town departments on the local government frequency. There is a
waiting area for people desiring to see an officer, or for those seeking permits or reports. The Police Department
secretary has more room to do her work away from the general line of traffic. There is a private office for the Chief
of Police; duty officers have a place to write reports adjacent to the records; and there is garage space for one
cruiser.
On the lower level, officers have space for their lockers and there is an evidence room which canbe locked
up, and also a storage room. The two Sergeants have ample room for their desks, andthere is privacy for interroga-
tions and interviews in either the Sergeant's Room or the combination Patrolmen's and Conference Room.
A separate police facility with all the advantages mentioned above has been sorely needed for a long time.
Thanks to the vote of the Townspeople, the perserverance of the Building Committee, the extra effort extended by
the architect and contractors, and the generosity of local businessmen, it has come to be. An Open House held on
November 27, 1977 was well attended by the public.
In 1977, 5,750 calls for service were received by the Police Department. These included 117 breaks into
private homes and businesses, 196 larcenies of personal property, and 41 stolen vehicles. The total amount of all
property stolen in Lunenburg amounted to $198,771.59 and the recovery of stolen property equalled $103,250.00
or a 48% recovery record.
Investigated were 316 accidents, 33 hit and run (both property damage and personal injury), 163 acts of
vandalism and 34 assaults. Also, there were 710 house checks made, 214 summons served, 166 gun permits issued,
84 people taken into protective custody, and 475 alarms (false or otherwise) responded to. As a result of stopping
1,633 motor vehicles, 521 citations were issued.
Some other activities requiring police investigation or assistance were drug complaints, child abuse and ne-
glect, sudden deaths and suicides, violation of Town By-laws and a variety of other complaints handled as part of
routine police duties.
At the end of May, Patrolman August N. Misner was forced to retire because of health reasons. Gus's ded-
ication to his profession, and his relationship with the young people of our community is missed. Patrolman Stanley
W. Barney was named as his replacement.
On June 8th, the Police Mutual Aid Pact between the communities of Fitchburg, Leominster and Lunenburg
was signed. Essentially, this Pact now gives police mutual powers when in neighboring towns, upon request.
To more effectively meet the demands for police service, additional manpower is being recommended to the
Board of Selectmen. To improve operation of the Department, adoption of written rules and regulations for police
officers is also recommended. To take advantage of the Mutual Aid Pact, the installation of a base station for
police only is suggested. A frequency for this has been issued by the Federal Communications Commission, and
all mobile and portable radios are equipped to use this frequency.
The Police Department consists of the following personnel:

Fire Department
In 1977 the Fire Department answered 430 calls for
assistance as follows: Rescue Unit Calls 197; Service
Calls 52; Mutual Aid 50; Brush Fires 32; Car Fires 20;
Needless Calls 20; Building Fires 12; Chimney Fires 17;
Electrical Fires 10; Accidents 10; False Alarms 3; Oil
Burner Calls 2; Accidental Alarm 1; Bomb Call 1; Rail-
road Car 1; Town Dump 1; Dumpster 1. This is approxi-
mately a 15% increase over last year's 376 calls. The
categories with the largest increases were Mutual Aid,
Service Calls and Chimney Fires. The activity within the
Department is increasing to the point where is difficult
to operate with only one full-time man.
The new fire truck ordered in 1975 and delivered
in June of 1977 has proven very satisfactory and is the
Fire Department's first diesel powered engine.
The new Rescue/Ambulance purchased in July 1977
was put into service in December and, needless to say,
has many advantages over the old one. Lunenburg's Res-
cue/Ambulance operation is still a two squad, call sys-
tem and is still proving to be very effective.
The other fire trucks, a 1961 FWD pumper and a
1966 FWD pumper-tanker, are still in good condition.
The Fire Chief recommends that the 1958 forest fire truck
James P. Deming, Fire Chief
be refurbished at an approximate cost of $4000 in hopes
of extending its usefulness for another ten years, rather than facing the replacement cost which would be approxi-
mately $25,000.
During the year training sessions were held for firefighters and rescue unit personnel. Firefighters were cer-
tified as "First Responders" and six Emergency Medical Technicians were recertified. Emergency Medical Technicians
are required to be recertified every two years.
I would like to thank the officers and members of the Department fortheir fine cooperation throughout the year.

Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District
At the close of oursixth year of operation, our school received an additional five years accreditation from the
New England Association of Schools and Colleges, making a total of ten years accreditation through 1985. "The
Commission action indicates a high degree of satisfactionwith progressrealized since the school's evaluation in 1975.
With eighty-seven percent (87%) of the recommendations now identified as either Completed or In Progress and an-
other three percent (3%) Planned for the Future, clearly the staff and administration at Montachusetthave responded
appropriately to visiting committee suggestions. The Commission reviewedwith interest many significant changes
that have occurred within the past two years to include the conversion of some facilities to accommodate specific
learning activities and relocation of other subject areas to better implement the school's philosophy."
For the first time, the Regional Vocational Technical Schoolswere notified of their entitlement to Title I Pro-
grams. The School Committee authorized the Administration to develop a proposal for use of such funds for educat-
ional Remedial Programs in the Title I Area in Reading and Math within the Academic Curriculum.
Our facility continued to have a high enrollment in all areas with 1,049 high school day students; 108 Allied
Health post-graduate students and 17 Early Child toddlers. The Adult Education Evening Program accommodated
1, 246 adults; the Summer Program enrolled 245 adults and the Afternoon Program from 3PM to 5PM had 138 students.
The Program Advisory Committees have continued to be exceptionally active and their work and interest is
sincerely appreciated as it has resulted in the continued success of the school.
Montachusett Regional was one of fourteen secondary schools which participated in a Pilot Program with the
Digital Corporation and the State Department of Education with a summer teacher training program and equipment
being received from Digital. A cooperative Data Processing Program with Mount Wachusett Community College in-
cluded collaborative programs for the Fall Term in Data Processing, Computer Operation and Programming. During
the Spring Term, a cooperative program was conducted in Graphics and Design.
Student activities continued at an active pace and with many participants from all courses. The Drama Club
presented "Fiddler on the Roof" which was one of the most successful events of the year. Social Studies students
traveled to the United Nations in New York City; the annual Winter Carnival was again success; and the
Commercial Art Department painted an 8' X 17' mural at Montrath of Groton, ahospital for severely handicapped
children. Carpentry students constructed 14 tent platforms for the Nashua Valley Council of the Boy Scouts of
America. Students from Carpentry, Electrical and Air Conditioning shops participated in the House Building Pro-
gram. These are just a few of the many constructive enterprises performed by our students.
George L. Ross, Superintendent Director, was severely and critically injured by a hit-and-run driverin
Ellenville, New York in July. Mr. Ross was hospitalized in Albany, New York for more than a month and has been
recuperating at his home since that time. He is expected to return to work in March of 1978. Thomas F. Markham,
Jr., Coordinator of Area/Continuing Occupational Educational Programs, was votedby the School District Committee
on August 3, 1977 to be Acting Superintendent - Director.
At the annual reorganization, Mr. Robert G. Raboin, of Fitchburg, was elected Chairman of the School Com-
mittee and Mrs. Janice U. Corser, of Royalston, was unanimously elected Vice-Chairman.
A number of commendations were awarded to the School, particularly our effort in the United Fund with SUC-
cessful participation by Staff and Administration; our participation in various civic activities; the Blood Bank which
is held in our nursing facility; visits to nursing homes and hospitals; and participation in community sponsored fairs.
With our additional five-year accreditation, the efforts of our Program Advisory Committee, our General Ad-
visory Committee, the School Committee, Administration, Staff and students, we at "Monty Tech" have again com-
pleted a year of proud achievements and accomplishments in all educational areas.
Lunenburg's elected representative to the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District Com-
mittee is Mr. Charles F. Valera.

Treasurer
Financial crises engulfing some of the nation's largest cities have stimulated greater concern about the credit-
worthiness of municipal debt, which for decades had been viewed as "risk free". A recognized need for closer atten-
tion to the handling of government funds in order to moderate these adverse trends has resulted in radical changes in
the emphasis placed on municipal finance and cash management, a rapidly changing area in which the City or Town
Treasurer plays a key role.
Effective cash management is generally recognized as essential to good fiscal management. This is particu-
larly true as mounting costs have placed ever-increasing pressures on local revenues, and we must find ways to meet
increased costs while placing a minimum additional burden on the taxpayer. Many of the policies and procedures that
are only now being introduced to many local governments for use in investing so-called "idle cash" (money that is
available in the treasury but is not needed for current day-to-day financial transactions) have been employed in our
Town for many years, and in Fiscal 1976 and Fiscal 1977, for instance, resulted in total interest earnings in excess of
$125,000. Even so, the Treasurer/Collector's office is in the process of reviewing and improving collection, deposit
and disbursement procedures to assure even greater availability of funds to meet cash needs and for investment pur-
poses. The development of procedures which bring revenue dollars into the treasury as quickly as possible and keep
those dollars aslong as possible can net significant increases in investment dollars earned. The following graph dem-
onstrates the degree to which an expanding cash management program in our Town has and will offset Town Office
expenditures, which have been increased appreciably in connection with the conversion to a fiscal year, with its
double tax billing procedures, and which have been further inflated to carry out a carefully supervised investment
program requiring considerable time and thoughtful administration. As can be seen, even with additional personnel
and higher wage levels requested for Fiscal 1979, the projected net cost of running the Town Office in Fiscal 1979
is $15,518 less than the all-time high for Calendar 1973, and only $3,906 more than the net cost in 1970.

It has been written (tongue-in-cheek no doubt) that current investment programs would undoubtedly be more
effective and efficient if a nineteenth-century practice were still in vogue: treasurers were allowed to keep as per-
sonal income any interest that might be earned on invested public dollars. The public as a whole did not realize the
benefits of such efficiency (that is, vested interest as an incentive), and the practice fell into disrepute with grow-
ing awareness that the interest earnedon invested public funds is public money justas much as the principal invested.
Still, one of the biggest problems with cash management in cities and towns is that the communities do not have the
capabilities to implement such program, which requires capable personnel, compensated accordingly (the vested
interest principle strikes again), and the result of such short-sightedness is the substitution of a "seat of the pants"
approach to investments, In Lunenburg, we have been fortunate in having policy-makers (Finance Committee, Per-
sonnel Committee, etc.) who recognize that it does cost money to make money, and who have been willing to auth-
orize staffing the Town Office with personnel capable of doing the job right. Some states produce an annual compar-
ative evaluation of cash investments and tax collections on a population basis. Were such an evaluation to be made
in Massachusetts, Lunenburg would rank exceedingly high.
A combined Treasurer's, Collector's andAccounting office facilitates Lunenburg's cash management program.
At the present time, taxes and Treasurer's receipts are being deposited into a regular savings bank on a daily basis,
-36-
===PAGE 39===
and are converted into higher-yield securities as quickly as possible. Separate "zero balance" checking accounts are
maintained for payrolls and other payments, with transfers being made into these accounts as checks are disbursed.
Very often, if securities are not neededon the day they mature, repurchase agreements are executed for short periods
of time, thus permitting higher yields than would be effected if the monies were placed in regular savings for the same
period.
The cover page of the Town Office Report (page 35) contains a sample cash flow chart (month of February,
1978), which has been included as an example of the type of information-gathering which is being utilized in order
to make accurate investment decisions. Estimates of specific dates of receipts and disbursements to determine cash
availability and develop an investment strategy are also referred to as cash budgets. A cash budget answers the ques-
tions: "how much" cash is available, "when" and "for how long?" Included on the final page of the Town Office
Report (page 83) is the Town Treasurer's somewhat ambitious plan for further improvements in Lunenburg's cash man-
agement program and the financial management of our Town.
Believe it or not, Mary,
cash managers
are also people.

===PAGE 68===
Tax Collector
It goes without saying that without good tax collections, the Cash Management program discussed in the Treas-
urer's report would not amount to much. In the very near future, all persons with past due property bills will receive
the following schedule of Tax Takings, As these dates will be strictly adhered to, delinquent taxpayers are urged to
contact their mortgaging banks immediately or take such other steps as will assure compliance.
Tax Taking for failure to pay all Fiscal 1977 taxes:
May 10, 1978
Tax Taking for failure to pay all Fiscal 1978 taxes:
October 3, 1978
Tax Taking for failure to pay all Fiscal 1979 taxes:
June 23, 1979
The State has also speeded up the processing of Motor Vehicle Excise, which means that many individuals who were
heretofore accustomed to receiving bills for vehicles registered on January 1 some time in the fall or even later, will
already have received their bills for 1978 by the time this report is distributed. Judy Anderson and Posey Carey are
pictured below commencing to process a voluminous box of excise bills received from the State, while Shirley McBride
and Sharon Guertin receive and process payments.
NAM
Town Clerk
Information recorded by the Town Clerk on a continuing basis is valuable in predicting growth trends on which
long-range master planning is based. These plans in turn become the basis for estimating future cash expenditures for
capital projects.

ARTICLE 4. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XVof the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of
the Town of Lunenburg" by inserting in Section 2 after the words "by popular election", the words ",all police officers under the
rank of Police Chief," and by deleting any and all references to the Police Department and/or police officers under the rank of
Police Chief as follows: (a) Delete from Section 16. HOURS OF WORK, Subsection III.A.; (b) Delete from Section 17. PAID VA-
CATIONS, first sentence, the words excepting Police special detail and off-duty court appearance,"; (c) Delete the last para-
graph (sentence) of Section 18. PAID HOLIDAYS; (d) Delete from the first sentence of Section 19. SICK LEAVE the words ",ex-
cluding Police special detail or off-duty court appearance time", delete the third paragraph in its entirety, and add the following
paragraph at the end of said Section 19: "An employee who resigns in good standing shall receive compensation for maximum of
60 days accumulated unused sick leave as follows: 5 years continuous service 10%; 15 years continuous service - - 50%; 25 years
continuous service - 75%. In the event of an employee's death, the above benefit will be paid tohis beneficiary. "; (e) Add after
Section 19 new section to be known as Section 19A as follows: "SECTION 19A. PERSONAL DAYS Two personal days may be taken
in any one twelve month period. Personal days shall be taken with the approval of the Department Head. "; (f) Delete the last para-
graph (sentence) of Section 21. MILITARY LEAVE; (g) Delete from Section 22. JURY DUTY the words "excepting Police special
detail or off-duty court appearance time, "; (h) Delete the last sentence of Section 23. LONGEVITY PAY; (i) Delete from Section
24. TRAVEL REIMBURSEMENT the words provided, however, that based on the 12¢ per mile figure, Police Officers providing
their own transportation to Superior Court in Worcester shall be reimbursed in the amount of $6.00 per trip"; and (i) Delete Section
25. CLOTHING ALLOWANCE BENEFITS; or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.

ARTICLE 8. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Earth Removal Ordinance contained in Article 1, subsection 7 (c), of

ARTICLE 9. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Earth Removal Ordinance contained in Article 1, subsection 8, of
Section 9 (of Article IX) of the Town By-laws by inserting the following new subsection to be known as section 8 (b), or take any
action relative thereto:
"Section 9, Article 1, section 8 (b)
The Board may grant a permit after it has made written findings as follows:
1. That the applicant has submitted adequate information upon which to base a decision, including as a minimum,
the requirements set out in Article 1 section 7 (c).
2. That the Board receive recommendations from the Planning Board, Board of Health and Conservation Commission,
regarding the impact on the environment from the proposed activity, and the Board shall not act until these three
Boards have submitted written reportsthereon or thirty days shall have elapsed following such request without receipt
of such reports.
-75-
===PAGE 78===
3. That the plan of reclamation submitted by the applicant is adequate to reclaim the land and the applicant has
provided and given sufficient to the Town event the applicant is unableto completethe plan of reclamation.
4. The Board has designated the land area in which removal operations are to be conducted. The Board shall not
allow a permit to conduct removal operations on more than 5 acres of land in any one year."
MOTION WHICH DID NOT INCLUDE PARAGRAPHS NUMBERED 3 AND 4 WAS DEFEATED.

enburg, entitled "Lot Size Regulations", for the purpose of protecting the Watershed Area, so that the required lot size in Residence
B will be 80,000 square feet, or act anything thereon. VOTED (347 AFFIRMATIVE, 47 NEGATIVE).

MENTS TO INCREASE THE CAPABILITY OF SUCH DEPARTMENTS TO PROTECT THE LIVES, SAFETY, AND PROPERTY OF THE

SERVICES, SUPPLIES, MATERIALS, CONTRACTUAL SERVICES, AND EQUIPMENTWHEN THE RESOURCES NORMALLY AVAILABLE
TO ANY MUNICIPALITY IN THE AGREEMENT ARE NOT SUFFICIENT TO COPE WITHA SITUATION WHICH REQUIRES POLICE
ACTION."

Suggestions for Improving Cash Flow
S
ome of the following suggestions for improving cash flow require only changes in present procedures which the Treasurer, giv-
en more time and the manpower requested in this year's budget, can implement with relative ease. Other suggestions need the co-
operation of other municipal officials, which should not be too hard to come by, as interdepartmental cooperation in Lunenburg
runs high.
CASH RECEIPTS
Speed up property tax payments by moving property tax takings forward
Faster and more frequent follow-up in contacting delinquent taxpayers
ACCOUNTS PAYABLE
More interaction with department heads in scheduling of accounts payable based on availability of funds
More emphasis on precise time for releasing checks so as to gain one or two days additional float
Investigate pros and cons of payroll period extensions
BANK ACCOUNTS
Reduce number of bank accounts to make it easier to identify idle cash for investment, and simplify mechanics of investing
Where additional bank accounts are used to enhance bank relations, negotiate for nominal balances
Investigate procedures whereby bank reports daily cash balances to enable the use of float
INVESTMENT INCOME
Fine-tune cash budget to permit increased investment income by making longer-term investments rather than planning to
roll over short-term investments
Arrange for overnight or weekend investment when heavy cash receipts or matured investments result in high cash balances
at the end of a day

No School Signals - 1977-78
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with
the Highway Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen
emergency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise
their personal judgment as to the wisdom of sending their children to school.
The following schedule of NO-SCHOOL SIGNALS will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS of TWO (2) BLASTS on the horn at the CENTRAL FIRE STATION indicates
no school at the following times for the groups indicated:
6:30 AM
Grades 8 through 12
7:15 AM
Grades Kindergarten through 7
10:00 AM
P. M. session, Kindergarten
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of Radio Stations WEIM and WFGL,
Fitchburg; WLMS, Leominster, as well as WTAG and WSRS (Stereo 96) in Worcester; and WBZ in Boston,
as promptly as the decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone
calls as rapidly as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center
or bus contractors might not be notified on time. Parents can help by not allowing their children
to make unnecessary calls. Your cooperation and understanding will be appreciated by all
concerned.
Report of Attendance Officer
Roger St. Jean
