Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
It is a privilege to submit my Eighth
Annual Report as Superintendent of Schools
in Lunenburg.
INTRODUCTION
This year I am varying my normal
annual report format and will deal in con-
siderable detail with only one issue--
grade reorganization. Annual reports of
the other school administrators, however,
have been so designed to present a review
of significant happenings in our schools
during 1977. I encourage you to read these
reports since they cover a range of impor-
tant topics, issues, and events that made
1977 a memorable year.
A report on grade reorganization has
been prepared for parents and was distrib-
uted at a recent public hearing. This
report contains a series of details and
recommendations that will have a profound
effect on the future direction of public
education in Lunenburg, particularly as it
relates to any future reorganizations or
new building construction. Major changes
Dr. William R. Holland
in curriculum and in staffing patterns are
also included in the report.
Since the Lunenburg School Committee has recently approved this grade reorganization plan
with implementation scheduled for next September, it is important to present the following detailed
grade reorganization report as part of this annual message.
GRADE REORGANIZATION
I. BACKGROUND RATIONALE
In the spring of 1976 the Lunenburg School Committee approved the following recommendation:
"Effective with the 1978-79 school year, the 8th grade will be relocated in the
Middle School and the 5th grade will be relocated in the Passios Elementary School. The
kindergartens and first grades will be relocated in the Elementary Annex. The 3rd grade
would move from the Annex to the Passios School. The position of Annex supervisor will
be eliminated with these duties to be assumed by the Elementary Assistant Principal.
"A plan of implementation which would include any revisions in the program of stud-
ies for the 5th and 8th grades, schedules, the recommended building reassignment of
staff, and recommendations for needed building modifications le. 9., removal of walls,
carpeting, folding partition) should be drawn up and presented to the school committee
prior to January 15, 1978.
"In 1978-79, the High School and Middle School students should ride the same buses
and be on similar time schedules."
The plan to reorganize grades in Lunenburg schools has been made feasible by the steady and
gradual decline of pupil enrollment since 1972. The factors contributing to this decline have
been well documented and discussed in past studies both locally, regionally, and nationally. It
is estimated that by September 1978, Lunenburg pupil enrollment will have dropped by 222 students
- 89
===PAGE 92===
over the 1972 level. With fairly stable conditions predicted, future enrollment projections as
calculated in 1977 by Dr. John Marshall, educational consultant, call for a further decline of
another 200 students between 1978 and 1982. By 1982, it is expected that our pupil enrollment
will be approximately 1800 students. Through the 1980's pupil enrollment should begin to level
off and then gradually start to increase.
The major reasons for grade reorganization are as follows:
1. We need to remove 8th graders from the high school. This move is long overdue.
Certain academic and social problems are created in a grade 8 to 12 building.
With 8th graders in the middle school, many of their present group identity and
social pressure problems will be eliminated. Academic scheduling and academic
credit problems will also be alleviated.
2. From a growth and development standpoint, a K-5, 6-8, 9-12 grade reorganization
represents a more effective mixture and arrangement of students. The research
indicates strong support for this type of organization. Also, such a grade
organization serves as a good base-line for the future if pupil enrollment
should begin a sharp incline. Building additions or new schools could then be
planned around the K-5, 6-8, 9-12 sequence. The new grade organization also
provides flexibility, in that, depending upon future enrollments and available
space, grade 5 can-either continue in the Passios School or be shifted to the
middle school as part of a grade 5 through 8 organization.
3. Long-range, the housing of the kindergartens and first grades at the Annex has
certain educational advantages. The smaller, more intimate school building
should be a positive factor in making the initial school experience for children
a successful one. Also, much can be said for creating an early childhood educa-
tion center in one building where first grade and kindergarten teachers can work
cooperatively and strive for greater individualization and personalization of
instruction. If enrollment at this level should surprisingly increase in future
years (it is expected to sharply decline), the Annex can easily be expanded and
modified to handle this contingency.
II. ENROLLMENTS AND PHYSICAL RELOCATION
For purposes of projection, the October 1, 1977 enrollment figures have been used to figure
space and program/scheduling needs in 1978-79. Based on these figures, K-12 enrollment next year
is expected to number 2004, a decrease of 50 students over this year.
Under reorganization, it is anticipated that pupil enrollments next year will be distributed
in the following manner:

III. TIME SCHEDULE
The following time schedule is proposed for Lunenburg schools in 1978-79:
TIMES
CHANGE OVER 1977-78
High School
7:50 AM - 2:10 PM
Unchanged
Middle School
8:00 AM - 2:10 PM
- 15 minutes
(8:25 AM - 2:40 PM this year)
Elementary School
8:45 AM - 2:45 PM
- 20 minues
(8:35 AM - 2:55 PM this year)
Two-thirds of the parents who responded to a time schedule survey in the spring of 1977 indi-
cated their desire to see the school day for elementary students shortened. Agreeing with a
majority of elementary teachers, parents felt that after factoring in long bus rides, the elemen-
tary student day (which in Lunenburg exceeds the state minimum by nearly an hour) was physically
too long for most children. Parents also expressed disapproval of shortening the high school day
(which exceeds the state minimum by only 26 minutes) with a major objection being a concern with
picking up high school students at bus stops in the dark if the 7:50 AM starting time were moved
back.
With reorganization, the high school and middle school students will ride the same buses
(this automatically shortens the middle school day) while the elementary (K-5) students will be
bused separately. Numerically, and for reasons of cost and staff scheduling, this appeared to be
the best alternative. (The school committee voted this change last spring.)
Several other points were considered in making this recommendation. They were:
- 91 -
===PAGE 94===
1. The loss of 15 minutes per day in the Middle School will only result in the loss
of 7 minutes of instructional time per day or 1 minute per class.
2. The loss of 20 minutes per day in the Elementary School will result in the loss
of 10 instructional minutes per day.
3. Lunch periods in both schools will remain unchanged.
4. The teachers work day will remain the same, i. e., 7 hours and 5 minutes in all
schools. The shortening of the Elementary and Middle School student day will,
however, result in teachers remaining after school from 15 to 20 minutes longer
than currently. This adjustment will dovetail nicely with several reorganization
"after-school" proposals that will be presented later in this report.
5. All schools will still bank sufficient time for early dismissal days. Under the
new time schedule all schools will still exceed the state minimum time require-
ments as follows:
SCHOOL
EXCEEDS STATE MINIMUM BY:
High School
-
26 minutes per day
Middle School
-
30 minutes per day
Elementary School
1
30 minutes per day
IV. TRANSPORTATION
The large majority of our bus routes were rebid this year. * These routes are rebid for a
three-year period.
Reorganization plans required school committee approval of the following bids and bid changes:
1. Thirteen (13) regular routes of forty miles per day
2. Three (3) single routes and three (3) "late bus" routes (to be combined with
single routes)
3. Elementary students will ride alone
4. Middle School and High School students will ride together
One major change being proposed is the request for three (3) "late buses.' One of these
buses would make a double run thereby giving us four separate runs to four separate corners of the
town. Late buses would be scheduled from Monday through Thursday beginning at 3:45 PM and would
not result in any additional mileage cost over this year's budget allotment.
Major reasons for the need for late buses are:
1. We plan to run an after-school personal development/intramural program at the
Middle School from 2:15 to 3:45 PM.
2. There will be a need to schedule one 5th grade instrumental music practice after
school at least one day per week.
3. With Elementary and Middle School teachers being required to spend more time
after school, this time can be used profitably by students and teachers for
individual help and make-up work.
*Because of declining enrollment one bus contract was terminated this past year.
- 92 -
===PAGE 95===
4. In the future, High School and Middle School pupils will not be able to ride
Elementary buses since there won't be space available. Since many High School
students currently ride Elementary buses when they stay after school, some way
will be needed for these students to get home.
V. PERSONNEL
A. Personnel Recommendations, 1978-79:
The following are 1978-79 personnel recommendations and have a direct impact on the
salary portion of the FY-79 School Budget -

NET SAVINGS:

Rationale:
Lack of classroom space in the Elementary School and declining enrollment result in the
elimination of one 4th and one 5th grade teaching position in 1978-79.
Relocation of the 5th grade in the Elementary School enables us to more efficiently
schedule math aides next year, thus allowing us to cut back twenty hours on this time.
Mr. Finneron, our Elementary Assistant Principal, will assume a more direct responsi-
bility for the supervision of the Annex, thereby eliminating the need for an Annex super-
visor.
The request to add the equivalent of a full-time reading teacher in grades K-8 is in
keeping with last year's reorganization of reading and language arts. These added part-
time reading teachers will allow Mrs. Hallfors and Mrs. Fletcher (our full-time reading
specialists) time in the day to provide individual help to teachers and assist Dr. Rose
Feinberg, our new Director of Reading and Language Arts, in administering the reading
program.
Other reasons:
1. The relocation of the 5th grade in the Elementary School will require additional
help in order to keep reading class sizes at present levels.
2. With reorganization, Mr. Angevine, high school reading specialist, will now be
able to concentrate on reading needs in grades 9 through 12, heretofore somewhat
neglected since 80% of his time has been spent with 8th graders.
3. Finally, with the addition of a new communications (reading) course in grade 7
and a new life skills (reading) course in grade 8 next year, both Dr. Feinberg
and Mrs. Fletcher will need to work very closely with classroom teachers at
these levels.

===PAGE 96===
B. Staff Reduction and Additions (1975 - 1978):
Since 1972, Lunenburg's pupil enrollment has declined by a total of 172 students. With
an estimated loss of another 50 students next year, our pupil decline during a six-year
period is expected to total 222 students. From 1975 to 1978, the professional staff has
been reduced while at the same time we added, shifted, and reorganized staff to meet new
state mandates and improve the quality of educational services. The following is a
breakdown of this fluctuation in staff.

VI. CURRICULUM IMPLICATIONS
There are several changes created by reorganization that will have a significant effect upon
our present curriculum. These changes are necessitated by either (1) declining course enrollment;
(2) lack of facilities; (3) conflicts in scheduling; (4) the emergence of other needs and priori-
ties; (5) a sounder philosophical rationale; or (6) new opportunities to make program improvements.
The changes are as follows:
1. A formal program in Industrial Arts and Home Economics will no longer be offered
5th graders. If feasible, we plan to incorporate many of the home and industrial
arts skills and concepts in a new Friday activity program for 4th and 5th
graders.
- 94
===PAGE 97===
2. Instrumental Music will continue to be offered 5th graders; however, because of
scheduling conflicts, the format will be different. A double period of instruc-
tion will be provided once a week; however, additional practice time will have
to be scheduled after school at least one afternoon per week. If our music
staff is unable to be scheduled for this after-school responsibility under their
contractual obligations (i. e., 7 hour and 5 minute school day), the after-
school session will be treated as a personal development activity (similar to
the Middle School proposal) and instructors will be paid an hourly rate. The
after-school session would also be contingent upon the availability of late
buses.
3. The formal foreign language program (Spanish and French) currently in 7th grade
will be dropped. It is recommended that this program be shifted to 8th grade
and that four years of foreign language be offered in grades 9 through 12.
Declining enrollments and relatively small class sizes in foreign language
classes during the past few years make this move feasible. With a total of five
years of foreign language still available for students, it will still be possible
for accelerated language students to complete the 5th level of our language pro-
gram. In recent years with six years of language experience available for all
students, there have been an insufficient number of students electing a Spanish
V and French V. (According to our policy, in order to justify an approved
course at least twelve (12) students must elect it.) Although it is anticipated
that there may be enough students to justify a Spanish V class next year, future
prospects do not look good since the High School, similar to the Elementary
level, is shortly about to feel the effects of declining enrollment.
Because teaching loads appear to be relatively light among foreign language
teachers in the High School, it is also recommended that thought be given next
year to scheduling available High School language teacher(s) to 7th grade
communications classes. Working cooperatively with Dr. Feinberg and 7th grade
teachers, the foreign language teacher could teach a unit in foreign language
readiness and an orientation to a foreign language and culture. Such an orienta-
tion would provide 7th-graders with a brief introduction to the study of another
language and provide some basis for their selection of French or Spanish in 8th
grade.
4. Eighth grade athletic programs (girl's and boy's basketball) as currently organ-
ized will be eliminated. Budget money for these programs will be transferred to
the new after-school personal development program at the Middle School. Eighth
graders will be allowed to try out for ninth-grade teams only where there are
openings, e. g., football, track, cross country. At no time will an eighth
grader be allowed to take the place of a ninth grader on a freshman athletic
team. If a need can be demonstrated a "modified" eighth grade basketball program
will be incorporated into our after-school format.
5. An afternoon "personal development" program will be offered to all Middle School
students. The program will run from 2:15 PM to 3:45 PM, Mondays through Thurs-
days. Late buses will be needed to run this program successfully. "Personal
development" implies more than athletics. It is our plan to include crafts,
performing arts, industrial and home arts workshops and clubs, along with intra-
murals. The program would be headed by a director/teacher and would utilize
community resource persons along with faculty. Instructors would be paid on an
hourly basis for their services.
The specific offerings and schedules for the personal development program would
be developed this spring by a special Middle School committee. No additional
funding is contemplated to run this program, since the combination of Middle
School intramurals and eighth grade athletic budget monies will be sufficient to
cover costs.
6. The reassigning of High School teachers to eighth grade assignments in the
Middle School will provide scheduling flexibility next year in the High School.
This flexibility will be utilized to implement our plan for a formal and well-
organized High School health education program--a major school system priority
- 95
===PAGE 98===
and a need identified by parents in our community survey of 1975. Next year,
Ms. Joan Callahan, our health education teacher, will devote a minimum of three-
fifths of her schedule to health education. In addition to teaching a required
health course to all ninth graders, Ms. Callahan, assisted by our Health Educa-
tion Committee, will organize a series of health seminars for all High School
students. If feasible, we would also like to provide Ms. Callahan with time to
coordinate health education activities K-12 and to have her actively supplement
home economics and physical education courses. Further thinking is to develop a
health resource center in the High School next year, where information and
individual counseling will be available for students in areas such as alcohol
and drug abuse, venereal diseases, smoking, pregnancy, and other health-related
issues.
7. A new course entitled "Communication Skills" will replace the foreign language
and special reading program currently in grade seven. This course will consist
of an expanded reading program for all seventh graders. Students will be homo-
geneously grouped according to communication skill ability on nine (9) levels
and the emphasis will be on the functional application of reading skills, stress-
ing the purposes of reading. Vocabulary, comprehension and interpretation,
silent reading, critical reading, using information, and rate of reading will be
the basic skills covered. The curriculum of this program will not duplicate
activities in the English program which currently emphasize writing, speaking,
listening, and literature. Units of instruction utilized by teachings in teach-
ing communication skills will be in areas such as career and consumer education,
newspaper reading and writing, and in special interest projects. As previously
mentioned, a unit on orientation/readiness in foreign language could also be
taught in this time block. And finally, consideration will also be given to
administering a basic competency test to seventh graders during the course of
the year.
8. A "Life Skills" course will be offered eighth graders who do not take a foreign
language. This course simply represents a different concept from the reading
course taught in this time slot now. The focus of life skills will be on the
application of reading and other skills to real-life situations. The materials
used will differ from those used in seventh grade and be more directly related
to practical life skills such as completing job applications, health and safety,
economic education, social and familial responsibilities and decision-making.

VII. FACILITIES
With the fifth grade transferring to the Passios School and the kindergartens and first
grades relocating in the Elementary Annex, there is the need for some interior renovations in these
buildings. Our basic need is to create two additional classrooms in the Passios School and two
additional (kindergarten) classrooms in the Annex. This can only be accomplished by shifting
existing spaces around and making maximum use of available space. After careful cost and struc-
tural analysis the following scheme is recommended:
Passios School - (Estimated Cost
= $3277.)

ELEMENTARY SCHOOL

classrooms.

1. Convert current stage area and teachers' room to new kindergarten classroom.
2. Convert present art room to new kindergarten classroom.
3. Convert custodial supply room to small group conference/learning area.
4. Convert nurse's room and women's restroom to new teachers' room.
5. Renovate present girls' and women's restrooms.
6. Construct combination gym pads and portable stage in gym/auditorium.
The total estimated cost, therefore, for remodeling new teaching spaces in the Passios and
Annex next year is $7563. This is a very reasonable figure, since our own maintenance staff is
scheduled to provide over 80% of the labor cost.
VIII. FINAL COMMENTS
The recommendations contained in this report were not made in haste but represent the careful,
collective thinking of many staff members. A full year was purposely set aside to thoroughly study
and plan for reorganization. Input was sought from administrators, teachers, and parents.
In addition to faculty and student planning and orientation sessions this spring, special
orientation programs on next year's changes will be held with parents of next year's kindergarten
students, first graders, and fifth graders. Parents of next year's eighth graders will receive a
similar orientation. These orientation sessions will be conducted by Elementary and Middle school
administrators and staff.
- 97 -
===PAGE 100===
As with most reorganizations, our first year should be considered a transition year. Future
refinements, rescheduling, program and staffing changes, and building modifications may have to be
made. We also know that space on the Elementary level will be at a premium for the next two years,
particularly in the Annex. However, in spite of a few minor drawbacks, we strongly feel that the
recommendations contained in this proposal are sound ones and will benefit the large majority of
Lunenburg students for many years to come.
CONCLUSION
In concluding this report, I would like to pay special tribute to three individuals who have
been of tremendous personal help and assistance to me.
The first individual is the late Tom Passios. With the passing of Tom, Lunenburg schools
lost a dear friend and helpful consultant. His contributions to the tradition of excellence our
schools enjoy will always be with us.
The second individual is Floyd Barney. This past fall, the Lunenburg staff honored Floyd in
recognition to his thirty years of outstanding service to Lunenburg schools. His professional
concern and dedication, his high personal standards, and that delightful sense of humor continue
to flourish.
A special thanks must also go to John Ludwig, our school committee chairman, who is leaving
the committee after four years of public service. In spite of great personal sacrifice and with a
deep sense of personal commitment, John has played an important leadership role during his tenure
on the school committee. His insightfulness and sense of fairness, his deep personal interest in
the schools, and overall astuteness will be sorely missed.
My genuine thanks go to school committee members, my fellow administrators, teachers, and
non-teaching staff members for their support suring the past year. Special thanks go to my secre-
tary, Mrs. "Ginny" Welch, and to Bob Okerman, our Supervisor of Buildings and Grounds, who are
directly responsible for the high quality and efficiency of our business personnel and maintenance
operations. And finally, to those people who actively support our schools, particularly those
persons who volunteer their time and service, thank you for your continued help and assistance.

Report of Special Needs Director
PURPOSE OF CHAPTER 766 OF THE ACTS OF
1972 OF THE COMMONWEALTH OF MASSACHUSETTS
"The General Court finds that past develop-
ment of special education programs has resulted
in a great variation of services to children with
special needs with some children having a greater
educational opportunity than others in less
favored categories or environments
In
light of the policy of the Commonwealth to pro-
vide an adequate, publicly supported education to
every child resident therein, it is the purpose
of this act to provide for a flexible and uniform
system of special education program opportunities
for all children requiring special education; to
provide a flexible and nondiscriminatory system
for identifying and evaluating the individual
needs of children requiring special education;
requiring evaluation of the needs of the child
and adequacy of the special education program be-
fore placement and periodic evaluation of the
benefit of the program to the child and the nature
of the child's need thereafter; and to prevent
denials of equal educational opportunity on the
basis of national origin, sex, economic status,
race, religion, and physical or mental handicap
in the provision of differential education
Floyd Benitz
services.
"
from: REGULATION 766, MASSACHUSETTS
DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION;
October 1, 1975; Page II.
It is often said that changes in the rights of persons (human rights) in our society during
this century and attitudes concerning these changes have been brought about by the actions of the
courts and legislature rather than through the educational processes of schools and churches.
Certainly, there will be differences of view on where the major stimulus for change has originated.
In any case, the General Court of the Commonwealth has acted to assure the equal educational
opportunity of persons regardless of physical or mental handicap and, wherein citizens disagree
with special education legislation, the law can be changed by the same kinds of processes and
efforts as in changing any other law.
WHO ARE THE CHILDREN AND YOUTH RECEIVING SPECIAL SERVICES?
In general, the special needs population in Lunenburg is like that of other communities
though we have no children identified as blind or profoundly deaf at the present time. We have
young children in the age range of 3 years to 8 years unable to walk and talk. In addition to
their nursery activities they receive speech therapy and physical therapy. Children with repaired
cleft palates require speech correction. Some of these children have been left with problems as a
result of birth injury, childhood diseases or accident.
Physical differences in children's bodies must be dealt with as we teach but must not be a
basis for not providing educational opportunity. Other children have problems from causes as yet
unclear to medical science, and many of our learning disabilities youngsters continue within that
99
===PAGE 102===
realm of medical and educational inquiry. Why does a child of normal or even gifted intelligence
have substantial difficulty in reading, spelling or handwriting? Oftentimes, current educational
practices assist this kind of child in compensating for deficits of which the origin is unknown.
The Special Needs child is the child regressing from a terminal illness, the child in a
wheelchair, the child with a substantial learning difficulty and the child with an emotional
problem.
We receive all children in the public schools and we are expected to "prevent denials of
equal educational opportunity on the basis of
physical or mental handicap in the provision
of differential education services.'
HOW ARE STUDENTS IDENTIFIED FOR SPECIAL SERVICES?
Children's problems may initially be discovered by teachers, parents, therapists or
physicians. A Core Evaluation Team (c. E. T.) referral is to be made on any child whose physical
or mental condition may substantially affect school performance.
Generally, the C. E. T. is comprised of an administrator, classroom teacher, school nurse,
psychologist, and learning specialist. Assessments are made of a student's strengths and problem
areas. The team includes the parent at the time of sharing information and developing an educa-
tional plan.
HOW ARE CHILDREN REGISTERED AS HAVING SPECIAL NEEDS?
The determination of special need results from the decision of a Core Evaluation Team. It
is not surprising that most children who are presenting problems as a reason for being referred
are, subsequently, found to have special needs for services. The study of the child is an indi-
vidual matter and the Core Team may conclude that some children have no special needs or need
monitoring as a minimum of service. Some mild special needs may be substantial in the view of the
team while other needs are not.
A student-centered educational plan is developed by the Core Evaluation Team and the parent.
The child's name is entered on the special needs register when the child receives services. The
distribution within the district for a three-year period is as follows:
October 1975
October 1976
October 1977

The increase is a direct result of the implementation of the law and of a child study or
evaluation process as required by regulation. The out-of-district placements for severely
impaired and low incidence children was 12 students in 1977.
WHAT ARE THE SERVICES AVAILABLE?
The Lunenburg Special Needs programming serves children and young people ages 3 through 21
years and encompasses the following: education required by state and federal laws, pre-school
screening, child search, clinical nursery, CORE evaluation, mental health consultations, psycho-
logical and learning assessment, speech therapy, prescriptive Special Needs, resource rooms,
alternative education, Special Needs Collaborative Programs, in-service training, pre-vocational
and vocational services, work/study, out-of-district placements, Special Needs transportation,
home and hospital programs.
- 100 -
===PAGE 103===
DEVELOPMENTS IN SPECIAL EDUCATION -
Pre-school screening and early intervention on behalf of special needs children are now usual
educational practices. Families should no longer feel isolated or helpless in obtaining adequate
care and education for their special needs child.
Children receive special school services as young as nursery school age. In the public
schools, study teams maintain a close watch for students who need more support.
Each child's progress is reviewed bi-monthly and a qualitative report is sent to the parent.
The child has a new plan made each year.
The transition in promotion to the next school has been improved. Work is underway with stu-
dents now as early as their seventh grade year to enhance their remaining years of school and
their employment in relation to a work/study program.
With special needs emphasis, Project Greenthumb will continue to provide alternatives for
some high school students. Business education, industrial arts and possibly a food service pro-
gram will improve options for more students in the future. Monty Tech will receive more serious
consideration as an alternative by eighth-grade special-needs students who will choose that option
as a new beginning rather than entering Lunenburg High School.
While the information required for the Special Needs Register will show another enrollment
increase next year, the students in a monitored status will cost relatively little in comparison
with the gains for these children and youth.
The Special Needs Advisory Council (S. N. A. C.) is comprised of parents, teachers and resi-
dents working together to further the education of children with special needs. S. N. A. C. has
sponsored informative public programs this year and invites the participation of interested
parties.
The percentage of state reimbursement to the town for special education has increased this
year. Similarly, the increase in special needs enrollment will triple the number of dollars of
entitlement from Federal funds for 1978-79.
We invite your visit to the special needs programs and with people providing services. In
the spring we will be training small groups of volunteers to assist us in our work. In addition,
we are looking for senior citizens and "grandparents" who are young in heart. We can put your
experience and help to work for children. Please call the office at 582-7751 for further informa-
tion.
CONCLUSION -
There are a few children whose learning abilities we may not change. That reality is to be
expected in any human service. However, we can help children to grow in self-esteem and personal
worth. The importance of those qualities is clear in job success and in life adjustments. It is
our goal to assist students in becoming socially responsible and economically independent. That
goal is most assured when the home and school share the responsibility with the student.
Finally, much appreciation is due the skilled and dedicated special needs staff who work with
children during school hours and conscientiously meet when parents are available before school and
in the evening. New staff members have joined with administrators and teachers in improving
special education programs. Virginia Abbott, Barbara Kondig, Karen Melanson and Isabella Cataldo
have brought their services to the Elementary School, and Gail DesBois is employed in the Middle
School. Also, we appreciate faculty members who have returned to the Lunenburg schools in new
capacities;t include Janet Maki and Sandra Trader in the Elementary School and Arnold Holtberg
in the Middle and High Schools. Barbara Brown, Katherine DeMar, Gloria O'Brien and Rachel Steele
now assist Elementary School special-needs pupils in the regular classroom on a part-time basis.
On behalf of the children, parents and residents, I want to thank the staff for their work and
cooperation throughout the year.

SCHOOL EVALUATION -
The faculty and staff of Lunenburg High
School have undertaken preparations for the
school to be evaluated by the New England
Association of Schools and Colleges in the
spring of 1979. Lunenburg High School has
been an accredited member of this association
since 1954, and continued membership requires
a school to undergo a self-study and an eval-
uation every ten years.
A Steering Committee, chaired by Mrs.
Josephine Meloon, with other representative
faculty members and administrators, has been
appointed. This committee is responsible for
coordinating the self-evaluation, establishing
a schedule for self-study and the outside
team of educators' visit, hearing reports from
staff sub-committees and making preparations
for the twenty-five member visiting team
which will spend three days at the school
examining the curriculum, staff, facility,
philosophy and the community's educational
- Richard J. Carey -
provisions. The team will determine the
PRINCIPAL
extent to which the teaching and learning
activities within Lunenburg High School carry
out its major purposes.
The New England Association of Schools
& Colleges is an accrediting association of
over 1100 public high schools, colleges,
independent private schools and vocational
technical institutions. Its purpose is to
assist members in improving the quality of
education within its institutions through a
process of objective evaluation and accredi-
tation.

ACADEMICS -
The Class of 1978 has received recognition for outstanding scholastic achievement this year
from the National Merit Scholarship Corporation, similar to the recognition accorded to the Class
of 1977. As a result of tests administered in 1977 to over a million high school juniors in all
fifty states, two of our students were recognized as semi-finalists, placing them in the top half
of one percent of all the senior class students in public and private schools within the Common-
wealth of Massachusetts. Upon the school's recommendation based upon four years of academic
achievement, in all probability these students will become finalists. The students so honored for
their scholastic application, interest, and attitude are
RICHARD BOURSEY
and
ROGER TAYLOR
In addition, seven other students received Letters of Commendation as a result of their scores
on the same battery of tests. This recognition placed these students among the top two percent
of all secondary school seniors throughout the country. This number of students so recognized for
two consecutive years from a public high school of our size is extremely significant. These out-
standing accomplishments bring great credit to the students themselves, the school staff, and the
community for providing the opportunity to reach these academic heights.
Also of note is the fact that sixty-two percent of last year's class are pursuing further
studies and twenty-seven percent have joined the world of gainful employment.

PROJECT GREENTHUMB -
A
TEAM
EFFORT
Project Greenthumb is an exciting new program with a "split" personality. Students alternate
study in two different areas: greenhouse management and forestry. During any given marking
period, one-half of the students enrolled in Project Greenthumb study conservation and applied
forestry techniques with Mr. Douglas Fleming; the other one-half study horticulture and green-
house management with Mr. Charles Tarleton, teacher, and Mrs. Linde Sacksen, aide. At the end of
each marking period, the students switch sections. The program is made possible through Title
IV-C, which provides Federal funds for innovative programs in education.
Project Greenthumb provides an alternative to general biology for high school sophomores, and
allows an individualized plan for selected students from other grades. During the morning students
are enrolled in courses of a traditional length, including English, Record-keeping, and an elective
subject. During the afternoon, three class periods are spent in Project Greenthumb.
Some of the highlights of the Greenthumb plan include:
(1) Our own mini-bus for field trips and transportation to work experiences.
(2) Training and use of student leaders to assist in supervising work experiences
and demonstrating proper techniques.
- 105 -
===PAGE 108===
(3) Our own school greenhouse, a twenty-eight-by-sixty-foot structure erected by
Robert Okerman with the coordinated efforts of the buildings and grounds crew,
the staff and students of Greenthumb, and local contracting specialists.
(4) Service-to-the-community projects, ranging from beautification of school
grounds, tree planting, and planning of foundation beds, to work performed for
various town departments, such as clearing cross-country ski trails and nature
trails, installation of trail markers, and construction of wooden bridges.
(5) Personalization of the educational plan through small group instruction,
apprenticeships, and work/study assignments with local agricultural businesses.
(6) Provision for retail sales experience (using a cash register, bookkeeping,
serving customers) through the student operated cooperative.
Meeting the needs of a wide range of students, Project Greenthumb was developed by a team of
our own educational leaders, local citizens and area representatives from agricultural enterprises.

CHURCH IN THE MEADOW - Munich, Germany '77

The Lunenburg Music Aiders, parent support group for the young people involved in the music
program in our schools, must be highly commended for the complete success of their gigantic
endeavor in raising sufficient funds to defray more than one-half the expense of sponsoring one
hundred and four student musicians on a concert tour of Germany. Official greetings were brought
to the Mayor of Luneburg, Germany, and concerts were presented by the band, high school chorus and
concert choir in schools and cathedrals throughout Germany during the week of April 14 - April 23,
1977. The musical tour of Germany was perhaps one of the biggest and most exciting events in the
history of Lunenburg High School. Such an undertaking required a lot of time and hard work on the
part of many people. To name active participants would indeed be risking the error of serious
omission. However, the Lunenburg Music Aiders have asked that a copy of their official card of
acknowledgment be included in this report in order that any citizen in the community who contrib-
uted in any way to this project may receive a sincere thank you.

The large number of parents who attended was most gratifying. We thank the many people who
made the journey to the Middle School and participated in this "Know Your School Night."
REORGANIZATION -
The faculty and administration have devoted a great deal of time this year to the reorganiza-
tion of grades for the 1978-79 school year. On the surface it would appear to be a simple matter
to transfer grades within our various schools. However, the educational, social and emotional
needs of children vary considerably with different age groups. Changes in curriculum, bus trans-
portation, starting and closing times, extra-curricular activities, transfer of teachers, size of
furniture for different age students, and different guidance and special education needs are only
part of the challenge we face with reorganization. At this point, we feel that many of the prob-
lems with reorganization have been resolved extremely well and only a few minor problems remain.
The Middle School personnel are looking forward to the challenge of having grade eight with
us next year, but we will certainly miss the grade five students and faculty.
MIDDLE SCHOOL ACTIVITIES -
There is insufficient space to give a detailed report of all activities offered the students
and community, but I would like to highlight a few major Middle School activities:
1. Mt. Watatic Ski Program
Again this year we are offering a ski program every Tuesday afternoon starting on
January 3. The students leave at 2:30 PM and return at 6:30 PM. Ski lessons are
offered to beginners at a very reasonable cost and rental equipment is available.
I would like to thank the faculty members and the Parent-Teachers Organization mem-
bers who have offered to serve as chaperones. At this time we have fifty-five
students taking part in the ski program.
2. Mini-Courses
Starting on January 30, many faculty members have offered to conduct a mini-course
during the third term. Students will be allowed to elect one mini-course if they
do not have a scheduling conflict. We also have some parent volunteers joining us
once a week for mini-course offerings. Some of the courses offered are math clubs,
music appreciation, beginning chess, arts and crafts, rocketry, macrame and intro-
- 109 -
===PAGE 112===
duction to foreign language. We feel that mini-courses give students a change of
pace from the basic academic subjects and further enrich their learning.
3. Dance Lessons
Mrs. Margot Gillis again this year conducted dance lessons for grade seven students.
There were approximately one hundred students in the ballroom dance program during
the eight weeks of lessons.
At the conclusion of the lessons, a regular dance was held at which the students
could show their parents and Mrs. Gillis what they had accomplished during the past
eight weeks.
We also have a debt of gratitude to Mr. Frank Cincotta for again assisting Mrs.
Gillis with the lessons.
4. Field Trips
All grade six students had the opportunity to visit the Museum of Fine Arts in
Boston during the month of October and view the extensive Egyptian collection. The
trip was arranged by Mr. Bishop as part of a social studies unit, since grade six
students study the early Egyptian culture quite extensively.
Other field trips for grade five and seven students will be arranged later in the
year.
COMMUNITY SERVICES -
The Middle School students must be congratulated for carrying out two very successful services
for the citizens of Lunenburg this school year.
Under the direction of Mr. Larry Bennett and other fifth-grade teachers, the fifth-grade stu-
dents conducted a canned-food drive and collected over 2540 cans of food, four turkeys, and a sum of
money, which were turned over to the Community Council for distribution to the needy at Thanks-
giving time.
For the first time this year, the sixth-grade students, under the direction of Mr. Jeff O'Neill
and the other sixth-grade teachers, conducted a mini-flea market in the Middle School library
before Christmas. A great deal of time on the part of students and teachers went into this pro-

M
ject, but it was well worth their efforts because $346.53 was turned over to the Community Council,
which helped make Christmas a lot happier for some of the less fortunate.
I am happy to report the above activities because too often we hear only about the over-
indulged younger generation.
CONCLUSION -
The success of any school system can be measured by the commitment on the part of parents,
teachers, school administrators, school committee members and other townspeople who want quality
education for our young people.
I believe all of the above mentioned have exhibited this commitment, for which I am most
grateful.

IN MEMORYOF
- THOMAS C, PASSIOS -
WHO DIED ON JUNE 27, 1977
ELEMENTARY PRINCIPAL, 1953 - 1966
AN OUTSTANDING EDUCATOR ON THE STATE,
REGIONAL, AND LOCAL LEVELS AND A
TRUE FRIEND AND ACTIVE SUPPORTER OF
LUNENBURG SCHOOLS
- 112 -
===PAGE 115===
November 20, 1966
Mr. Thomas C. Passios
Highland Street
Lunenburg, MA 01462
Dear Mr. Passios:
Today represents a uniquely proud and special time in your life
and that of your family as you receive the appreciative recognition
of the citizens of Lunenburg for your thirteen (13) years of out-
standing service to the community and to the education of its chil-
dren. As school committee members who have worked closely with you,
we are keenly aware of the constant dedication you brought to your
assignment as Principal of the Elementary Schools in Lunenburg. You
have, indeed, left behind elementary schools which bear the Tom
Passios' imprint of solid, progressive, educational progress.
Your positive influence on our elementary schools has been such
that the Lunenburg School Committee felt compelled to decide on some
lasting, suitable recognition of your efforts. As a result, on Mon-
day, October 31, 1966, the committee unanimously voted to name the
school in which we are now standing, the THOMAS C. PASSIOS ELEMENTARY
SCHOOL. Our sincere congratulations go to you on this occasion.
Signed:
LUNENBURG SCHOOL COMMITTEE -
Howard F. Brock, Chairman
Stanley H. Howard, Vice-Chairman
Marion M. Benson, Secretary
F. Clifton Miller, Member
Walter B. Keeler, Member

===PAGE 116===
Report of Elementary School Principal
"Excellence is not something a school
suddenly reaches. In our school, we are
constantly aiming for that one step beyond in
any program that will improve it for our stu-
dents. The pursuit of excellence is a con-
tinuing process-not one of static goals. We
are interested in goals, but more so, we are
interested in continuing goals and definite
actions toward the attainment of excellence."
THOMAS PASSIOS
1961 Lunenburg Town Report
The 1977-78 school year marks the 25th
anniversary of the Thomas C. Passios Elemen-
tary School. We are saddened by Tom Passios'
death this past summer but are encouraged and
strengthened by the challenge which Tom left
for this school, as well.as for public educa-
tion in Massachusetts. The above message taken
from the elementary school report in 1961
emphasizes the ongoing need to plan for the
- Paul E. Ralston -
future. We must recognize that children
PRINCIPAL
entering school today will, in most instances,
complete their formal education in the 1990's
and become the work force of the 21st century.
With mankind's knowledge base doubling
approximately every five years, the concept of
imparting only factual information or easily
measurable knowledge should be examined.
In our scenario of the future with
depleting energy supplies, increasing popu-
lations, declining food surpluses and changing
family and societal values, we must examine
the necessity of an educational effort that
not only stresses a knowledge base, but also
develops societal values and good common
sense.
In keeping with Tom's example, we are
constantly reevaluating our local educational
system and continuing to plan for the future.
ENROLLMENT STATISTICS -
The opening day enrollment figures for
the 1977-78 school year totaled 675 children
in kindergarten through grade four. This
represented a decline of 34 students from
last year's total population. Since a high
- Robert Finneron -
of 804 students in 1973-74 we have experienced
ASSISTANT PRINCIPAL
a decline of 129 students. This will result
- 114 -
===PAGE 117===
in the elimination of five classroom teaching positions by next fall. The sharp enrollment decline
at the elementary level has facilitated plans for reorganizing our grade structure within the
school system. Beginning next year, grade five will be moved to the Elementary School and grade
eight will become part of the Middle School. A more detailed explanation of reorganization plans
is outlined in the Report of the Superintendent of Schools.

PERSONNEL CHANGES -
In June of 1977 Mrs. Lillian Griffiths resigned from her position as Reading Director for
grades K-7 in the Lunenburg school system. Those of us who worked with Lil, and everyone who
benefited directly from her expertise and sensitivity, will truly miss her presence. We would all
like to express our thanks and appreciation for a job well done.
- Dr. Rose Feinberg was appointed to the position of Reading and Language Arts Director
for grades K-8 as well as Reading Director for grades 9-12. Dr. Feinberg has had
extensive experience including designing curriculum, teaching at the elementary and
graduate school levels, consulting, supervising staff, conducting workshops, and
writing for national publications.
- Mrs. Virginia Abbott has been employed as a half-time learning disabilities teacher.
Mrs. Abbott has had previous experience as a speech pathologist and learning disabil-
ity specialist at Burbank Hospital and in the school systems of Dracut, Gardner, and
Leominster.
- Mr. Mark Forgues was hired to a second grade teaching position. Since 1974 Mr. Forgues
has been head teacher in the Gateway program of the CAPS Collaborative in Gardner.
- Mrs. June Mueller was hired to the guidance counselor position. Mrs. Mueller has had
previous teaching experience at the elementary level, as well as counseling experience
in the Cushing Academy/Fort Devens PREP program.
- Debra Colello has been assigned to a Title I reading position. Ms. Colello has been
teaching sixth grade at the Turkey Hill Middle School for the past three years.
- Barbara Kondig has been employed,on a part-time basis, as a learning disability tutor.
Mrs. Kondig has worked in a similar capacity in the Leominster school system.
CONCLUSION -
I would like to thank the staff, parent volunteers, Superintendent of Schools, and School
Committee for their continued efforts to maintain Lunenburg's high educational standards.

Retirements
NELDE K. DRUMM - September 17, 1955 - June 30, 1977
In 1955, Mrs. Drumm was hired as an art teacher in
the high school on a one day per week basis. Her responsi-
bility was increased to three days per week beginning
September 1, 1957. On September 1, 1958, Mrs. Drumm was
appointed Art Supervisor for the system on a full-time
basis.
The current high quality of the school system's art
program can be contributed to Mrs. Drumm's diligent
efforts, dedication and leadership over the years. The
exceptional level of excellence attained by many of Mrs.
Drumm's past students also is a tribute to her and the
outstanding program she gave shape and direction to from
1955 to 1977.
LILLIAN S. GRIFFITHS
- September 1, 1941 - June 30, 1944
- February 6, 1950 - June 30, 1977
Mrs. Lillian (Szocik) Griffiths began her teaching
career in Lunenburg as a junior high teacher in the old
high school building (now the Annex) on September 1, 1941,
resigning in June 1944 to be married. She returned in
February 1950 as a grade two teacher in the old Center
School to complete the 1949-50 school year. On September
1, 1957 she was appointed as Supervisor of Reading and
Allied Subjects, the position she held until her retire-
ment.
During her thirty years of service as a teacher and
supervisor, Lil's qualities of human warmth and dedicated
interest in children and her continued deep professional
commitment, loyalty, and conscientiousness contributed to
her uniqueness as a professional. The current quality and
success of Lunenburg's elementary reading program can be
directly attributed to her outstanding efforts and skills.
Lil was honored last June by the entire Lunenburg school
staff at a testimonial held at the Old Mill. Current and
past faculty members and many of her friends were present
to pay tribute to this very special person.
BARBARA R. PEASE - October 24, 1960 - June 30, 1968
- September 1, 1968 - June 30, 1977
Mrs. Pease began her tenure in Lunenburg as a guidance
secretary in the high school. She resigned from this
position in June of 1968.
In September of 1968, Mrs. Pease was elected as a
home economics teacher at the high school, retiring in
June 1977 after nine years of service.
In addition to her duties as a home economics teacher,
Barbara served as an understanding confidante and counselor
to many of her students. Her pleasant and friendly
personality and cooperative professional attitude will be
missed.
- 121 -
===PAGE 124===
HAROLD K. CARRIER -
August 26, 1968 - August 19, 1977
Harold began as a junior custodian in the
Passios Elementary School. Effective January
1, 1971 he was appointed by the School Commit-
tee to assume the responsibility of senior
custodian at the Turkey Hill Middle School.
In accepting his intention to retire, the
School Committee expressed their appreciation
for his contributions to the school during his
nine years of service. His pride and care of
the Middle School, as well as his cooperative,
understanding leadership as President of the
Lunenburg Custodial Union, were two points of
appreciation stated by the Committee.
A retirement party was held by school
staff on September 15 for Harold at which time
they wished him "bon voyage" on his pending
trip to Hawaii and many happy years of retire-
ment.
In Memory of
ALBERT H. BALCOM
On February 13, 1977, A1 passed away in
Naples, Florida at the age of 77. For the past
few years he had been making his home in Tahini
Mobile Village, Estoro, Florida.
A1 began as custodian on October 12, 1953
in the then brand new Lunenburg Elementary
School. He remained at the Elementary School
until March 25, 1957 at which time he trans-
ferred to the Lunenburg Jr.-Sr. High School
and assumed the duties of Head Custodian. He
remained in this position until his retirement
On July 21, 1967, having completed fourteen
years of "devoted and conscientious service.' 11
During his tenure he also served as School
Attendance Officer.
He was survived by his wife, one daughter,
three grandchildren and five great-grandchil-
ren, and several nieces and nephews.

School Nursing Report
Lunenburg now has its own staff of nurses directly under the control of the school depart-
ment, effective this September, as a result of a change in providing community nurse coverage made
by the Board of Health.
Headed by Jean E. Hobbs, R. N. ,as full-
time coordinating nurse and nurse at the
Elementary School, the other two staff mem-
bers are Marie B. (Ginger) Richards, R. N.,
part-time at the Middle School, and Jean M.
Larkin, R. N., part-time at the High School.
Dr. Joel Neuschatz is the school physician,
with Dr. Gary Asher assisting with special
needs assessment physicals and as a member
of the Health Education Curriculum Committee.
To continue the focus of the school
health program on preventative health
measures and early detection of possible
health problems, the school nurses function
in many areas. In order to comply with state
regulations for immunizations required to
attend school, a complete check was made of
all health records, and parents were con-
(L-R) "Ginger" Richards, Jean
tacted in an effort to determine immuniza-
Larkin, Jean Hobbs
tion status. At clinics held in conjunction
with Nashoba Associated Boards of Health,
the following immunizations were given:
169 M-M-R (Measles-Mumps-Rubella) to grades K-6 and boys, grades 7-12; 59 Measles to girls, grades
7-12; 98 Tetanus-Diphtheria to grades one and eleven; and 47 Polio to grade one. The end result
of this activity was that all in-coming kindergarten students were immunized and many older stu-
dents brought up-to-date on their immunization series.
Also, through Nashoba and the school nursing staff, 63 Tine tests were done on first-grade
students, and 121 Mantoux tests were done on school personnel to certify them as being free from
tuberculosis. Students in grades two, five, and eight are able to participate in a dental flouride
program, and students in kindergarten and grade three may have their teeth checked by the dental
hygienist.
Vision and hearing screening is done annually on all students, as required by state regula-
tions. Parents are notified of failures, with referral forms to doctors returned to the school.
Physical examinations are required for all students entering school for the first time, for trans-
fer students, for all students involved in junior-senior high school competitive sports, for stu-
dents evaluated under Chapter 766, and for students with frequent absences and complaints. Hear-
ing and vision screening is done on all entering kindergarten students, and on any three or four
year old upon parent request. When necessary, conferences with parents are held in school or over
the telephone, with the nurses available at anytime to talk to parents who have questions or con-
cerns.
The postural screening program for students in grades five through eight, developed in 1977,
is continuing under the guidance of the Department of Public Health. The initial screening is
done in gym class, with re-screening by the school nurse. Parents are contacted if there is need
for further evaluation by a physician.
Both Mrs Hobbs and Mrs. Larkin are members of the Health Education Curriculum Committee, and
it is anticipated that in the future the school nurse will be more involved in health education on
a formal basis. However, every time a school nurse sees a child in the health office, there is an
informal session of health education. A Red Cross Multi-Media First Aid class was also taught to
grade-ten students by Mrs. Hobbs and Ms. Claire Friedrich, science teacher at the high school.
- 147 -
===PAGE 150===
The nurse's role in Chapter 766 is increasing. as are the number of students involved. Lunen-
burg nurses make home assessment visits to gather birth and develop mental history, and serve as
liaison with private physicians and as medical resource person in core evaluation team meetings.
To keep current with changes in the medical field and new programs available for the school
health programs, the nurses are members of the American School Health Association and the Massa-
chusetts School Nurses' Association. All three nurses attended a five-session school nurse seminar
in addition to a workshop on scoliosis screening, conducted by the Department of Public Health,
Central District, and have participated in various programs offered by the Massachusetts School
Nurses' Association, Fitchburg State College, and Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. Mrs. Hobbs
participated in a three-day workshop for "Mandated Reporters of Child Abuse and Neglect," pre-
sented by the Office for Children. In turn, a program was conducted for local school administra-
tors, guidance counselors, and nursing staff, involving representatives from the Department of
Welfare and police, on problems of child abuse and neglect, and procedures to be used in reporting
suspected instances.
In conclusion, the school nursing staff is pleased to be able to continue the excellent school
health program conducted in the past by the Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service and is looking
forward to expanding the health services offered students in the Lunenburg school system.
"Easy now, Mrs. Hobbs!"
